Three dimensional topological quantum field theory from U q ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( 1 | 1 ) ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)) and U ⁡ ( 1 | 1 ) U(1|1) Chern–Simons theory
Nathan Geer, Matthew B. Young

Abstract
. We introduce an unrolled quantization U q E ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( 1 | 1 ) ) U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)) of the complex Lie superalgebra 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( 1 | 1 ) \mathfrak{gl}(1|1) and use its categories of weight modules to construct and study new three dimensional non-semisimple topological quantum field theories. These theories are defined on categories of cobordisms which are decorated by ribbon graphs and cohomology classes and take values in categories of graded super vector spaces. Computations in these theories are enabled by a detailed study of the representation theory of U q E ​ ( 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( 1 | 1 ) ) U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)) , both for generic and root of unity q q . We argue that by restricting to subcategories of integral weight modules we obtain topological quantum field theories which are mathematical models of Chern–Simons theories with gauge supergroups 𝔭 ​ 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( 1 | 1 ) \mathfrak{psl}(1|1) and U ⁡ ( 1 | 1 ) U(1|1) coupled to background flat ℂ × \mathbb{C}^{\times} -connections, as studied in the physics literature by Rozansky–Saleur and Mikhaylov. In particular, we match Verlinde formulae and mapping class group actions on state spaces of non-generic tori with results in the physics literature. We also obtain explicit descriptions of state spaces of generic surfaces, including their graded dimensions, which go beyond results in the physics literature.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 81T45; Secondary 20G42.

Introduction

This paper constructs and studies new three dimensional topological quantum field theories from non-semisimple categories of representations of the unrolled quantum group of the complex Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
and establishes a relationship between these theories and various supergroup Chern–Simons theories studied in the physics literature. Before stating our results in more detail, we provide some context.

Background and motivation

Chern–Simons theory is a three dimensional quantum gauge theory which was introduced by Witten to give a physical realization of the Jones polynomial
[
Wit89a
]
. The input data is a compact Lie group
G
, the gauge group, and a class
k\in H^{4}(BG;\mathbb{Z})
, the level, satisfying a non-degeneracy condition. At the physical level of rigor, Chern–Simons theory produces invariants of links in closed oriented
3
-manifolds which are local in the sense that they can be computed using cutting and gluing techniques. Witten also argued that calculations in Chern–Simons theory can be made using its boundary conformal field theory, a Wess–Zumino–Witten theory with target
G
, thereby importing techniques from the theories of rational vertex operator algebras and affine Lie algebras to knot theory and
3
-manifold topology. Since the physical definition of Chern–Simons theory relies on path integrals, it cannot, at present, be used to give a mathematical construction of the theory. Motivated by this, Reshetikhin and Turaev constructed from a modular tensor category
\mathcal{C}
a three dimensional topological quantum field theory
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathsf{Cob}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}_{\mathbb{C}}
which, in particular, encodes invariants of
\mathcal{C}
-colored links in
3
-manifolds with the expected locality properties
[
RT90
,
RT91
,
Tur94
]
. When
G
is simple and simply connected, in which case
k
is an integer, there is a modular tensor category
\mathcal{C}(G,k)
of semisimplified representations of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
at a
k
-dependent root of unity
q
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}(G,k)}
is a mathematical model of Chern–Simons theory
[
RT91
,
And92
,
TW93
,
Saw06
]
. Crucial to the construction of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}
is that modular tensor categories are semisimple, have only finitely many isomorphism classes of simple objects and have the property that simple objects have non-zero quantum dimension.

Extensions of Chern–Simons theory to more general classes of gauge groups have been proposed in the physics literature. This includes gauge groups which are non-compact Lie groups and complex reductive groups
[
Wit91
,
BNW91
,
Guk05
,
DGLZ09
]
and Lie supergroups
[
Wit89b
,
Hor90
,
RS92
,
RS93
,
RS94
,
KS09
,
GW10
,
Mik15
,
MW15
]
. Such extensions are expected to have applications to many areas, including the Volume Conjecture, logarithmic conformal field theory and three dimensional quantum gravity. Mathematical constructions of these extensions have largely been obstructed by technical and conceptual difficulties which appear when moving beyond compact gauge groups. For example, the Chern–Simons/Wess–Zumino–Witten correspondence is fundamentally unclear in these extensions, thereby preventing the use of recent advances in the theory of logarithmic vertex operator algebras
[
GQS07
,
QS07
,
CR13
,
CMY22
]
. Since Chern–Simons theories with non-compact gauge groups involve categories of line operators which are non-semisimple, have infinitely many isomorphism classes of simple objects and have simple objects with vanishing quantum dimension, there are serious obstructions to applying the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction.

It is therefore of interest to extend Reshetikhin–Turaev-type constructions beyond modular tensor categories. Early approaches to such extensions are given in the works of Hennings
[
Hen96
]
and Kerler and Lyubashenko
[
KL01
]
. More recently, the first author and collaborators have created a theory of renormalized quantum invariants of low dimensional manifolds
[
GPMT09
,
CGPM14
,
BCGPM16
,
DRGPM20
,
DR22
]
. Central categorical structures of this theory include relative pre-modular categories, non-degenerate relative pre-modular categories and relative modular categories which produce invariants of links, invariants of closed
3
-manifolds and three dimensional topological quantum field theories, respectively. In this paper we focus on relative modular categories, the strongest of these structures, which are generalizations of modular tensor categories that allow for non-semisimplicity, infinitely many simple objects and simple objects with vanishing quantum dimension. Roughly speaking, a relative modular category
\mathcal{C}
is a ribbon category which is compatibly graded by an abelian group
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
and carries a degree
0
monoidal action of a group
\mathsf{Z}
. It is required that there exists a sufficiently small subset
\mathsf{X}\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
such that the full subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{g}\subset\mathcal{C}
,
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, are semisimple and have only finitely many isomorphism classes of simple objects modulo
\mathsf{Z}
. The associated three dimensional topological quantum field theory
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, constructed by De Renzi
[
DR22
]
, is defined on a category of admissible decorated three dimensional cobordisms and takes values in a braided monoidal category of
\mathsf{Z}
-graded complex vector spaces. When
\mathcal{C}
is in fact a modular tensor category, the theory
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}
reduces to that of Reshetikhin and Turaev. In general,
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}
enjoys many new features not shared by modular theories, including the ability to distinguish homotopy classes of lens spaces and produce representations of mapping class groups with interesting properties, such as having Dehn twists act with infinite order.

Categories of weight modules over unrolled quantum groups of complex simple Lie algebras are relative modular and their associated topological quantum field theories have been the subject of recent interest
[
BCGPM16
,
DRGPM20
,
CDGG21
,
DR22
]
. The case of unrolled quantum groups of complex Lie superalgebras is more subtle. For example, depending on the precise class of weight modules being considered, the Lie superalgebras
\mathfrak{sl}(m|n)
,
m\neq n
, produce categories which are relative modular or only non-degenerate pre-relative modular
[
Ha18
,
AGPM21
,
GPMR21
,
Ha22
]
. The resulting topological quantum field theories have not been studied. This paper presents the first systematic study of topological quantum field theories arising from quantum supergroups and suggests that examples arising from higher rank Lie superalgebras admit natural physical realizations, in contrast to the original expectations of Mikhaylov and Witten
[
MW15
]
. Further examples of (non-degenerate) relative pre-modular categories, some of which are conjectured to extend to relative modular categories, and their applications to knot theory and
3
-manifold topology can be found in
[
GPM07
,
GPM10
,
AGPM21
]
.

Main results

We construct new examples of relative modular categories using the representation theory of an unrolled quantization of the complex Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
. We study in detail the resulting topological quantum field theories and connect them to
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
and
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theories and
U(1|1)
Wess–Zumino–Witten theory, as studied in the physics literature by Rozansky and Saleur
[
RS92
,
RS93
,
RS94
]
and Mikhaylov
[
Mik15
]
. We also connect our work to mathematical results on the quantum topology of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)

[
FN91
,
KS91
,
Res92
,
Vir06
,
Sar15
,
BI22
]
. In the remainder of this introduction we outline the structure of the paper and state the main results.

We begin in Section
1
by establishing our conventions for relative modular categories and recalling how these categories can be used to define invariants of links and
3
-manifolds and, ultimately, three dimensional topological quantum field theories. Our first main result asserts finite dimensionality of the state spaces of these field theories under the assumption that the input relative modular category is TQFT finite in the sense of Definition
1.15
. TQFT finiteness is a relatively weak condition and is straightforward to verify in concrete examples. For example, a relative modular category which is locally finite abelian with finitely many projective indecomposable objects modulo
\mathsf{Z}
in each degree
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is TQFT finite.

Theorem A

(Theorem
1.16
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a relative modular category which is TQFT finite. Then for each decorated surface
{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
, the state space
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})\in\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is finite dimensional.

Theorem
A
provides a general reason for the observed finite dimensionality of state spaces in all known examples, namely those arising from relative modular categories of modules over unrolled quantum groups of complex simple Lie (super)algebras
[
BCGPM16
,
DRGPM20
,
AGPM21
,
Ha22
]
and those of this paper. Theorem
A
is proved by exhibiting an explicit, combinatorially defined spanning set of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})
using special
\mathcal{C}
-colorings of a fixed spine of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

In Section
2
we introduce a non-standard quantization
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
. The algebra
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
is an unrolled version of standard quantizations of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)

[
Kul89
,
KT91
,
Res92
]
, in the sense of
[
CGPM15
]
. Fix
q\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\pm 1\}
. The superalgebra
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
is generated by even Cartan generators
E
,
G
,
K^{\pm 1}
and odd Serre generators
X
,
Y
. The generator
E
should be viewed as a logarithm of
K
, but this relation is not imposed at the level of the algebra. Instead, we consider the category
\mathcal{D}^{q}
of all weight
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-modules on which
K
acts by
q^{E}
. Let also
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q}
be the full subcategory of weight modules whose
G
-weights are integral; no integrality of
E
-weights is assumed. A natural Hopf superalgebra structure on
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
gives
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
the structure of rigid monoidal categories. We study
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
in detail, obtaining complete descriptions of their simple and projective indecomposable objects. The culmination of our results in Section
2
is summarized as follows.

Theorem B

(Theorems
2.16
,
2.20
,
2.22
,
2.23
,
2.25
)
.

The categories
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
admit relative modular structures which depend on whether or not
q
is a root of unity and, if so, the parity of the order of the root of unity. In particular,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
are generically semisimple ribbon categories. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
are TQFT finite with respect to any of the above relative modular structures.

More precisely,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
admit relative modular structures for any
q\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\pm 1\}
, which we refer to as the case of arbitrary
q
, and admit second, distinct, relative modular structures when
q
is a root of unity. Denote by
\mathcal{C}
either of the categories
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
with any of the relative modular categories of Theorem
B
and by
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
the associated topological quantum field theory. In all cases, the braided category
\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a graded version of complex super vector spaces.

In the remainder of the paper we study in detail
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}
(Sections
3
-
5
) and their relationship to
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
and
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons and Wess–Zumino–Witten theories (Section
6
). To avoid cumbersome statements, in the introduction we state precise results only for
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}^{q}
with
q
a primitive
r
th
root of unity,
r\geq 3
odd. In this case, the category
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is graded by
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
, corresponding to
(E,G)
-weights modulo
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, with
\mathsf{X}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}\times\{\bar{0}\}
and
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. In the body of the paper we treat all cases of Theorem
B
.

Our first series of results concerns the
\mathsf{Z}
-graded vector space
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathsf{Z}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C},k}({\mathcal{S}})
assigned to a decorated surface
{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Part of the data of
{\mathcal{S}}
is a cohomology class
\omega\in H^{1}({\mathcal{S}}_{0};\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
on the underlying closed surface
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
of
{\mathcal{S}}
. The description of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})
simplifies considerably when
\omega
is generic in the sense that
\omega(\gamma)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
for some simple closed curve
\gamma\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
.

Theorem C

(Theorems
3.5
and
4.3
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a decorated connected surface of genus
g\geq 1
without marked points and with generic cohomology class
\omega
. For any
(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, the partition function of
{\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})}
, the closed decorated
3
-manifold obtained by crossing
{\mathcal{S}}
with
S^{1}
and extending
\omega
to
\omega\oplus(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})
, is

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})=(-1)^{g+1}r^{2g-1}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}(q^{\bar{\beta}+i}-q^{-\bar{\beta}-i})^{2g-2}.

Moreover, the partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})
and state space
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})
are related through the Verlinde formula

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})=\sum_{(n,n^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\chi(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q},(n,n^{\prime},\bullet)}({\mathcal{S}}))q^{-2r(\overline{\beta}n^{\prime}+\overline{b}n)},

where
\chi(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q},(n,n^{\prime},\bullet)}({\mathcal{S}}))
denotes the Euler characteristic of the
\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
-graded subspace of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})
consisting of vectors with
\mathsf{Z}
-degree of the form
(n,n^{\prime},\bullet)
.

Theorem
C
is proved using an explicit surgery presentation of trivial circle fibrations and the representation theoretic results of Section
2
. The strategy of proof is similar to its counterpart for topological quantum field theories arising from the unrolled quantum group
U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))

[
BCGPM16
]
. In the body of the paper we allow
{\mathcal{S}}
to carry marked points, in which case
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})
depends also on
\bar{b}
.

To obtain a more detailed understanding of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})
, we first prove in Theorems
3.3
and
4.1
that, in the present class of examples and under the assumed genericity of
\omega
, the general spanning set of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})
constructed in Theorem
A
can be reduced to a much smaller set. Theorems
3.3
and
4.1
can be seen as vanishing results, asserting that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})
is concentrated in a restricted set of
\mathsf{Z}
-degrees. Using these results, we prove that

\lim_{\overline{\beta}\rightarrow\bar{\frac{1}{4}}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}}).

Together with Theorem
C
, this leads to the following explicit description of state spaces of generic surfaces.

Theorem D

(Corollaries
3.7
and
4.5
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a decorated connected surface of genus
g\geq 1
without marked points and with generic cohomology class. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q},-k}({\mathcal{S}})
is trivial unless
k=(0,d,\bar{d})
for some
d\in[-(g-1),g-1]\cap r\mathbb{Z}
, in which case it is of dimension
r^{2g}{2g-2\choose g-1-|d|}
. In particular, the total dimension of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})
is

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})=r^{2g}\sum_{n^{\prime}=-\left\lfloor{\frac{g-1}{r}}\right\rfloor}^{\left\lfloor{\frac{g-1}{r}}\right\rfloor}{2g-2\choose g-1-|n^{\prime}|r}.

When the cohomology class
\omega
is not generic, the vector space
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})
is considerably more complicated. In this setting we restrict attention to the torus, where we again obtain a complete description of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})
. We prove that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})=\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C},0}({\mathcal{S}})
is two dimensional for arbitrary
q
(Propositions
3.9
and
3.11
) and that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})=\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C},0}({\mathcal{S}})
is
r^{2}+1
dimensional for
q
a primitive
r
th
root of unity (Proposition
4.6
). The result for arbitrary
q
is particularly surprising since its contrasts the conjectured behavior of TQFTs constructed from
U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))

[
BCGPM16
]
. We construct explicit bases of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{S}})
to prove the following result.

Theorem E

(Theorems
3.13
and
4.7
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a decorated connected surface of genus one without marked points and with non-generic cohomology class
\omega
. The mapping class group action of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})
admits an explicit description which, in particular, shows that the Dehn twist acts with infinite order.

Mapping class group actions with properties similar to those of Theorem
E
for arbitrary
q
are obtained using the representation theory of
U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
at a root of unity in
[
BCGPM16
]
.

For a given
q
, the theories
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
are closely related.
A priori
, the significant difference in the gradings of these categories- the grading group for
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
is much smaller than that of
\mathcal{D}^{q}
- could lead to significant differences in
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
. However, the constraints on the
\mathsf{Z}
-support of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
, as in Theorem
D
, show that this is not the case. In particular, Theorems
C
,
D
and
E
hold for the relative modular categories
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
, with essentially the same proofs.

Finally, in Section
6
we connect our results with the physics literature. When
q
is arbitrary, Proposal
6.1
states that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
is Chern–Simons theory with gauge Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
, the two dimensional purely odd Lie algebra, as studied by Mikhaylov
[
Mik15
]
. We observe that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
is effectively independent of
q
. This reflects the physical expectation that, because
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
is purely odd, there is no quantization of the level. If instead
q
is a primitive
r
th
root of unity, then Proposal
6.2
states that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
is
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory at level
r
, as studied by Rozansky–Saleur and Mikhaylov
[
RS92
,
RS93
,
RS94
,
Mik15
]
. More precisely, since
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
and
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theories arise as topological twists of supersymmetric quantum field theories
[
RW97
,
GW10
,
KS09
]
, they are expected to be examples of topological quantum field theories valued in derived or differential graded categories and we expect the theories
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
to be their homological truncations. Proposals
6.1
and
6.2
use in an essential way the group
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of global symmetries of these supergroup Chern–Simons theories which allows them to be coupled to background flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connections. As evidence for these proposals, we match the Verlinde formulae (Theorem
C
) and dimension formulae for generic state spaces (Theorem
D
) with physical predictions
[
RS93
,
RS94
,
Mik15
]
. We also match the mapping class group action of Theorem
E
with that obtained using
U(1|1)
Wess–Zumino–Witten theory
[
RS93
]
. Theorem
E
is very similar to mapping class group actions obtained using
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory
[
Mik15
]
and combinatorial quantization
[
AGPS18
]
; we give a precise comparison in Section
6.2
. The connection between
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
and the Alexander polynomial, discussed in Section
3.4
, matches the expected connection for
U(1|1)
Wess–Zumino–Witten theory
[
RS92
,
RS93
]
and
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
and
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theories
[
RS94
,
Mik15
]
. However, the results of this paper go beyond what has appeared in the physics literature. This includes the construction of a topological quantum field theory and an explicit description of the state spaces of generic surfaces of all genera. As discussed above, the final point is strictly stronger than the Verlinde formula in isolation.

Creutzig, Dimofte, Garner and the first author proposed in
[
CDGG21
]
that the topological quantum field theory associated to the relative modular category of weight modules over the unrolled quantum group
U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}(n))
admits a physical realization as the homological truncation of a topological
A
-twist of
3
d
\mathcal{N}=4
Chern–Simons-matter theory with gauge group
SU(n)
. According to Kapustin and Saulina
[
KS09
]
, Chern–Simons theory with gauge group a Lie supergroup can be realized as a gauged affine Rozansky–Witten theory and is therefore a
B
-twisted mirror of the theories studied in
[
CDGG21
]
. Implications of
3
d mirror symmetry at the level of topological quantum field theories are conjectured in
[
CDGG21
]
. The results of
[
BCGPM16
]
and this paper provide mathematical foundations and calculations for the
A
-sides and
B
-sides, respectively, of these conjectures. It would be very interesting to use these results to study concrete instances of these conjectures.

In a different direction, it would be very interesting to study the relationship between the topological quantum field theories of this paper, constructed from the representation theory of
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
, with decategorifications of the Heegaard Floer theory of
[
Man19
,
MR20
]
, constructed from the categorical representation theory of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)^{+})
.

1.
Preliminary material

Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed ground field.

1.1.
Ribbon categories

We refer the reader to
[
EGNO15
]
for background on monoidal categories.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category. Throughout the paper, we assume that the functor
\otimes:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is
\Bbbk
-bilinear, the monoidal unit
\mathbb{I}
is simple and the
\Bbbk
-algebra map
\Bbbk\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{I}),k\mapsto k\cdot\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{I}}
, is an isomorphism. If, in addition,
\mathcal{C}
is rigid, braided and has a compatible twist
\theta=\{\theta_{V}:V\rightarrow V\}_{V\in\mathcal{C}}
, then
\mathcal{C}
is called a
\Bbbk
-linear ribbon category
. The left and right duality structure maps are denoted

\overleftarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}:V^{*}\otimes V\to\mathbb{I},\qquad\overleftarrow{\mathrm{coev}}_{V}:\mathbb{I}\to V\otimes V^{*}

\qquad\overrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}:V\otimes V^{*}\to\mathbb{I},\qquad\overrightarrow{\mathrm{coev}}_{V}:\mathbb{I}\to V^{*}\otimes V,

respectively, and the braiding is
c=\{c_{V,W}:V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V\}_{V,W\in\mathcal{C}}
. The
quantum dimension
of
V\in\mathcal{C}
is

\operatorname{qdim}V=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{I}).

An object
V\in\mathcal{C}
is called
regular
if
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}
is an epimorphism.

Our conventions for diagrammatic computations with ribbon categories are that diagrams are read from left to right, bottom to top and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-9.71823pt}{2.2738pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

A morphism

f:V_{1}\otimes{\cdots}\otimes V_{n}\rightarrow W_{1}\otimes{\cdots}\otimes W_{m}
in
\mathcal{C}
is represented by the diagram

f

\scriptstyle{W_{1}}
\scriptstyle{...\hskip 1.0pt}
\scriptstyle{W_{m}}
\scriptstyle{V_{1}}
\scriptstyle{...\hskip 1.0pt}
\scriptstyle{V_{n}}

whose box is called a
coupon
. Following Turaev
[
Tur94
, §I.2]
, a
ribbon graph
in an oriented manifold
M
is a compact oriented surface embedded in
M
which decomposes into elementary pieces, consisting of bands, annuli and coupons, and is the thickening of an oriented graph. In particular, the vertices of the graph which lie in the interior
\mathring{M}=M\setminus\partial M
are thickened to coupons. A
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graph
is a ribbon graph whose (thickened) edges are colored by objects of
\mathcal{C}
and whose coupons are colored by morphisms of
\mathcal{C}
. The intersection of a
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graph with
\partial M
is required to be empty or consist of univalent vertices. In diagrams, we represent the induced framing of the core of the ribbon graph using the blackboard framing.

Associated to a
\Bbbk
-linear ribbon category
\mathcal{C}
is the
Reshetikhin–Turaev ribbon functor

F_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{C}}
is the ribbon category of
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,1]

[
Tur94
, Theorem I.2.5]
. Given a
(1,1)
-tangle
T_{V}
whose open strand is colored by a simple object
V\in\mathcal{C}
, define
\langle T_{V}\rangle\in\Bbbk
by the following equality in
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)
:

F_{\mathcal{C}}(T_{V})=\langle T_{V}\rangle\cdot\operatorname{Id}_{V}.

Two formal
\Bbbk
-linear combinations of
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graphs are called
skein equivalent
if their images under
F_{\mathcal{C}}
agree. The corresponding equivalence relation is denoted
\dot{=}
.

1.2.
Modified traces

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\Bbbk
-linear ribbon category.

Definition 1.1
.

An ideal in
\mathcal{C}
is a full subcategory
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{C}
which

(1)

is stable under retracts: if
W\in\mathcal{I}
and
V\in\mathcal{C}
and there exist morphisms
f:V\to W
and
g:W\to V
such that
g\circ f=\operatorname{Id}_{V}
, then
V\in\mathcal{I}
, and

(2)

absorbs tensor products: if
U\in\mathcal{I}
and
V\in\mathcal{C}
, then
U\otimes V\in\mathcal{I}
.

Since
\mathcal{C}
is ribbon, an ideal also absorbs tensor products from the left.

Example 1.2
.

An object
P\in\mathcal{C}
is called
projective
if every epimorphism
V\rightarrow P
admits a section. The full subcategory
{\mathcal{P}}\subset\mathcal{C}
of projective objects is an ideal.
∎

Let
V,W\in\mathcal{C}
. Recall that the right partial trace
\operatorname{ptr}_{W}:\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V\otimes W)\rightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)
is defined by

\operatorname{ptr}_{W}(f)=(\operatorname{Id}_{V}\otimes\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{W})\circ(f\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{W^{*}})\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{V}\otimes\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{W}).

Definition 1.3

(
[
GKPM11
, §3]
)
.

(1)

A
modified trace
(or
m-trace
) on an ideal
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{C}
is a family of
\Bbbk
-linear functions

\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V}:\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)\to\Bbbk\}_{V\in\mathcal{I}}
which satisfies the following:

(a)

Cyclicity property
:
\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V}(f\circ g)=\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{W}(g\circ f)
for all
V,W\in\mathcal{I}
and
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(W,V)
and
g\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,W)
.

(b)

Partial trace property
:

\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V\otimes W}(f)=\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V}(\operatorname{ptr}_{W}(f))
for all
V\in\mathcal{I}
,
W\in\mathcal{C}
and
f\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V\otimes W)
.

(2)

Given an m-trace
\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}
on
\mathcal{I}
, the modified dimension of
V\in\mathcal{I}
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V)=\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V}(\operatorname{Id}_{V})\in\Bbbk
.

1.3.
Relative modular categories

We recall a number of definitions from
[
CGPM14
,
DR22
]
.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a
\Bbbk
-linear category.

(1)

A set
\mathcal{E}=\{V_{i}\mid i\in J\}
of objects of
\mathcal{B}
is
dominating
if for any
V\in\mathcal{B}
there exist
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}\subseteq J
and morphisms
\iota_{k}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(V_{i_{k}},V)
and
s_{k}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(V,V_{i_{k}})
such that
\operatorname{Id}_{V}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\iota_{k}\circ s_{k}
.

(2)

A dominating set
\mathcal{E}
is
completely reduced
if
\dim_{\Bbbk}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(V_{i},V_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in J
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\Bbbk
-linear ribbon category and
\mathsf{Z}
an additive abelian group. We often view
\mathsf{Z}
as a discrete monoidal category with object set
\mathsf{Z}
.

Definition 1.5
.

A
free realisation of
\mathsf{Z}
in
\mathcal{C}
is a monoidal functor
\sigma:\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that

(1)

\sigma(0)=\mathbb{I}
,

(2)

\operatorname{qdim}\sigma(k)\in\{\pm 1\}
for all
k\in\mathsf{Z}
,

(3)

\theta_{\sigma(k)}=\operatorname{Id}_{\sigma(k)}\text{ for all }k\in\mathsf{Z}
, and

(4)

for any simple object
V\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
V\otimes\sigma(k)\simeq V
if and only if
k=0
.

We often identify a free realisation
\sigma:\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
with a collection of objects
\{\sigma(k)\}_{k\in\mathsf{Z}}
, omitting from the notation the monoidal coherence data
\sigma(k_{1})\otimes\sigma(k_{2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\sigma(k_{1}+k_{2})
,
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathsf{Z}
.

Remark 1.6
.

The definition of a free realisation was first given in
[
CGPM14
, §4.3]
with the condition that
\operatorname{qdim}\sigma(k)=1
for all
k\in\mathsf{Z}
. Definition
1.5
first appeared in
[
DR22
, §1.3]
.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
be an additive abelian group. A
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-grading on
\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence of
\Bbbk
-linear categories
\mathcal{C}\simeq\bigoplus_{g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}}\mathcal{C}_{g}
, where
\{\mathcal{C}_{g}\}_{g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}}
are full subcategories of
\mathcal{C}
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

\mathbb{I}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

(2)

if
V\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
, then
V^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{-g}
, and

(3)

if
V\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
and
V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{g^{\prime}}
, then
V\otimes V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{g+g^{\prime}}
.

It follows from the definition that if
V\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
,
V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{g^{\prime}}
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,V^{\prime})\neq 0
, then
g=g^{\prime}
. Note also that the full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subset\mathcal{C}
is ribbon.

Definition 1.8
.

A subset
\mathsf{X}
of an abelian group
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is

(1)

symmetric
if
\mathsf{X}=-\mathsf{X}
and

(2)

small
if
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}(g_{i}+\mathsf{X})\neq\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
for all
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
.

Definition 1.9
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
,
\mathsf{Z}
be abelian groups,
\mathsf{X}\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
a small symmetric subset and
\mathcal{C}
a
\Bbbk
-linear ribbon category with the following data:

(1)

a
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-grading on
\mathcal{C}
,

(2)

a free realisation
\sigma
of
\mathsf{Z}
in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, and

(3)

a non-zero m-trace
\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}
on the ideal of projective objects of
\mathcal{C}
.

A category
\mathcal{C}
with this data is called a
pre-modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
if it has the following properties:

(1)

Generic semisimplicity
: For every
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, there exists a finite set of regular simple objects
\Theta(g):=\{V_{i}\mid i\in I_{g}\}
such that

\Theta(g)\otimes\sigma(\mathsf{Z}):=\{V_{i}\otimes\sigma(k)\mid i\in I_{g},\;k\in\mathsf{Z}\}

is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{C}_{g}
.

(2)

Compatibility
:
There exists a bicharacter
\psi:\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\Bbbk^{\times}
such that

(1)

c_{\sigma(k),V}\circ c_{V,\sigma(k)}=\psi(g,k)\cdot\operatorname{Id}_{V\otimes\sigma(k)}

for any
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
,
V\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
and
k\in\mathsf{Z}
.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pre-modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
.

(1)

For each
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, the
Kirby color of index
g
is the formal
\Bbbk
-linear combination of objects

\Omega_{g}:=\sum_{i\in I_{g}}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V_{i})\cdot V_{i}.

(2)

For each
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
and
V\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
, the
stabilization coefficients

\Delta_{\pm}\in\Bbbk
are defined by the skein equivalences

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}

(3)

The pre-modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category
\mathcal{C}
is called
non-degenerate
if
\Delta_{+}\Delta_{-}\neq 0
.

As the notation suggests,
\Delta_{\pm}
are independent of
g
and
V\in\mathcal{C}_{g}

[
CGPM14
, Lemma 5.10]
.

Definition 1.11
.

A
modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
is a pre-modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category
\mathcal{C}
relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
for which there exists a scalar
\zeta\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, called the
relative modularity parameter
, such that for any
g,h\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
and
i,j\in I_{g}
the skein equivalence

(2)

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\vskip 17.22217pt

holds.

The relative modularity parameter satisfies
\zeta=\Delta_{+}\Delta_{-}

[
DR22
, Proposition 1.2]
. In particular, modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-categories are non-degenerate pre-modular.

1.4.
Invariants of closed
3
-manifolds

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a non-degenerate pre-modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
. The data
\mathcal{C}
was used in
[
CGPM14
]
to define invariants of decorated
3
-manifolds. We briefly recall this construction. Note that relative modularity in
[
CGPM14
, Definition 4.2]
is what is called here relative pre-modularity in Definition
1.9
. All manifolds in this paper are assumed oriented and compact.

A
compatible triple

(M,T,\omega)
consists of a closed connected
3
-manifold
M
, a
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graph
T\subset M
and a cohomology class
\omega\in H^{1}(M\setminus T;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
such that the
\mathcal{C}
-coloring of
T
is
\omega
-compatible
, in the sense that each oriented edge
e
of
T
is colored by a non-zero object of
\mathcal{C}_{\omega(m_{e})}
, where
m_{e}
is the oriented meridian of
e
. A surgery presentation
L\subset S^{3}
for
M
is called
computable
for
(M,T,\omega)
if one of the following conditions holds:

(1)

L\neq\emptyset
and
\omega(m_{L_{i}})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
for all connected components
L_{i}
of
L
.

(2)

L=\emptyset
and there exists an edge
e
of
T
with
\omega(m_{e})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
.

Let
R
be a
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graph. Suppose that at least one edge of
R
is colored by a generic simple object
V\in\mathcal{C}
and let
T_{V}
be the
(1,1)
-ribbon graph obtained from
R
by cutting an edge labeled by
V
. Properties of m-traces imply that

F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}(R):=\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V}(T_{V})=\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V)\langle T_{V}\rangle\in\Bbbk

is a well-defined invariant of
R
. See
[
GPMT09
,
GKPM11
]
. In
[
CGPM14
, §2]
it is shown that
F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
can be used to define a Reshetikhin–Turaev-type diffeomorphism invariant of triples
(M,T,\omega)
. See equation (
3
) below for a slight generalization.

1.5.
A topological quantum field theory extension

When
\mathcal{C}
is a modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
, the decorated
3
-manifold invariants of Section
1.4
is part of a once-extended topological quantum field theory (TQFT)
[
BCGPM16
,
DR22
]
. In this paper, we restrict attention to the truncation of this theory to a non-extended TQFT.

A
decorated surface

{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\{p_{i}\},\omega,\mathcal{L})
consists of

•

a closed surface
\Sigma
with a set
*
of distinguished base points, exactly one for each connected component of
\Sigma
,

•

a (possibly empty) finite set
\{p_{i}\}\subset\Sigma\setminus*
of oriented framed
\mathcal{C}
-colored points, where the coloring of
p_{i}
is an object of some
\mathcal{C}_{g_{i}}
,

•

a cohomology class
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus\{p_{i}\},*;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})\simeq H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus\{p_{i}\};\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
such that
\omega(m_{i})=g_{i}
, where
m_{i}
is the oriented boundary of a regular neighborhood of
p_{i}
, and

•

a Lagrangian subspace
{\mathcal{L}}\subset H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
.

A
decorated cobordism

\mathcal{M}=(M,T,\omega,m):{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
between decorated surfaces consists of

•

a cobordism
M:\Sigma_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma_{2}
,

•

a
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graph
T\subset M
whose coloring is compatible with that of the marked points of
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
,
i=1,2
,

•

a cohomology class
\omega\in H^{1}(M\setminus T,*_{1}\cup*_{2};\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
for which
T
is
\omega
-compatible and which restricts to
\omega_{i}
on
\Sigma_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and

•

an integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}
called the
signature defect
.

This data is required to be
admissible
in the sense that for each connected component
M_{c}
of
M
which is disjoint from the incoming boundary
\Sigma_{1}
, there exists an edge of
T\cap M_{c}
colored by a projective object or there exists an embedded closed oriented curve
\gamma\subset M_{c}
such that
\omega(\gamma)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
.

When it will not cause confusion, we refer to
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{M}
simply as surfaces and cobordisms, respectively. Decorated surfaces and their diffeomorphism classes of decorated cobordisms form a category
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Disjoint union gives
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
a symmetric monoidal structure.

Fix a square root
{\mathscr{D}}\in\Bbbk^{\times}
of
\Delta_{+}\Delta_{-}
and set
\delta=\frac{{\mathscr{D}}}{\Delta_{-}}
. Given a closed cobordism
\mathcal{M}=(M,T,\omega,m)
with computable surgery presentation
L\subset S^{3}
, define

(3)

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,T,\omega,m)={\mathscr{D}}^{-1-l}\delta^{m-\sigma(L)}F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}(L\cup T)\in\Bbbk.

Here
l
is the number of connected components of
L
,
\sigma(L)
is the signature of the linking matrix of
L
and each component
L_{i}
of
L
is colored by the Kirby color
\Omega_{\omega(m_{L_{i}})}
. Setting
m=0
in equation (
3
) recovers a scalar multiple of the decorated
3
-manifold invariants of
[
CGPM14
]
.

Let
\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\Bbbk}
be the monoidal category of
\mathsf{Z}
-graded vector spaces over
\Bbbk
and their degree preserving morphisms. Let
\gamma:\mathsf{Z}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\}
be the unique pairing which makes the diagrams

\hbox to235.66pt{\vbox to66.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 117.8307pt\lower-33.4691pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-114.4977pt}{-30.1361pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}.base}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\qquad k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathsf{Z}

commute. The vertical arrows are the monoidal coherence data of the free realisation. The pairing
\gamma
induces a symmetric braiding on
\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\Bbbk}
.

Theorem 1.12

(
[
DR22
, Theorem 6.2]
)
.

A modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category
\mathcal{C}
relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
with a choice
{\mathscr{D}}
of square root of
\Delta_{+}\Delta_{-}
defines a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\Bbbk}
whose values on closed cobordisms coincide with
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

The TQFT
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{Z}
if it will not cause confusion, can be described as follows. Given a decorated surface
{\mathcal{S}}
, let
\mathcal{V}({\mathcal{S}})
be the (infinite dimensional) vector space over
\Bbbk
with basis the set of all decorated cobordisms
\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}({\mathcal{S}})
the vector space with basis the set of all decorated cobordisms
{\mathcal{S}}\to\emptyset
. Define a pairing

\langle-,-\rangle:\mathcal{V}^{\prime}({\mathcal{S}})\otimes_{\Bbbk}\mathcal{V}({\mathcal{S}})\to\Bbbk,\qquad\langle{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},\mathcal{{\mathcal{M}}}\rangle={\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\circ{\mathcal{M}})

and let
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
be the quotient of
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{S})
by the right kernel of
\langle-,-\rangle
:

\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}):=\mathcal{V}({\mathcal{S}})/\text{kerR}\langle-,-\rangle.

The assignment
{\mathcal{S}}\mapsto\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
extends to a functor
\mathsf{V}:\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}_{\Bbbk}
which, however, is not in general monoidal. To remedy this, fix
g_{0}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
and a simple projective object
V_{g_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}_{g_{0}}
. For each
k\in\mathsf{Z}
, denote by
\widehat{S}_{k}
the decorated
2
-sphere

\widehat{S}_{k}=(S^{2},\{(V_{g_{0}},1),(\sigma(k),1),(V_{g_{0}},-1)\},\omega,\{0\})\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}

determined by the oriented colored points
\{(V_{g_{0}},1),(\sigma(k),1),(V_{g_{0}},-1)\}
and cohomology class
\omega
uniquely determined by compatibility. See
[
BCGPM16
, §4.5]
,
[
DR22
, §5.1]
. The TQFT state space of
{\mathcal{S}}
is defined to be the
\mathsf{Z}
-graded vector space

(4)

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathsf{Z}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\mathcal{S}})

where
\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\mathcal{S}})=\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
. Up to monoidal natural isomorphism,
\mathcal{Z}
is independent of
g_{0}
and
V_{g_{0}}
.

Lemma 1.13

(
[
DR22
, Lemmas 4.7 and 4.8]
)
.

The state space
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
satisfies the following properties.

(1)

Let
\Sigma
be the underlying surface of
{\mathcal{S}}
and
M
a connected manifold with
\partial M=\Sigma
. Then the cobordisms
\{\mathcal{M}=(M,T,\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}\}_{T,\omega}
span
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
.

(2)

A
\Bbbk
-linear combination
\sum_{i}a_{i}\mathcal{M}_{i}
of cobordisms
\mathcal{M}_{i}:\emptyset\rightarrow{\mathcal{S}}
is zero in
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
if and only if
\sum_{i}a_{i}\langle\mathcal{M}_{i},\mathcal{M}\rangle=0
for all cobordisms
\mathcal{M}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\emptyset
.

Although
\mathcal{V}({\mathcal{S}})
is infinite dimensional,
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is finite dimensional in all known examples; see Section
1.6
below. Moreover, Lemma
1.13
allows one to make finitely many computations to determine
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. The main tools used to do such computations are
\sigma
-equivalence
and
skein equivalence
, described in
[
BCGPM16
, §4.1]
and
[
DR22
, §4.1]
.

The
\mathsf{Z}
-grading of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
can be refined to a
H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathsf{Z})
-grading. This refinement, used in Sections
3.2
and
4.2
, is discussed for
\mathcal{C}
the category of weight modules over
U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
in
[
BCGPM16
, §4.7]
and can be generalized as follows. Given a decorated surface
{\mathcal{S}}
with underlying surface
\Sigma
and
\varphi\in H^{0}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
, let
\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\varphi}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{S}}
be the morphism obtained from the cylinder
\Sigma\times[0,1]
by adding to the pullback of the cohomology class of
{\mathcal{S}}
the unique class
\partial\varphi
which vanishes on cycles of
\Sigma\times[0,1]
and takes the value
\varphi(*)
on the path
*\times[0,1]
, where
*
is the basepoint of a connected component of
\Sigma
. The group
H^{0}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
acts on
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
by post-composition with
\mathsf{V}(\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\varphi})
. Denote by

\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{k}\in H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathsf{Z})}\mathsf{V}_{\mathbf{k}}({\mathcal{S}})

the associated spectral decomposition, where

\mathsf{V}_{\mathbf{k}}({\mathcal{S}})=\{v\in\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})\mid\mathsf{V}(\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\varphi})v=\big(\prod_{c\in\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}\psi(k_{c},\varphi_{c})\big)v\;\;\forall\varphi\in H^{0}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})\}

and we have identified
H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathsf{Z})\simeq\bigoplus_{c\in\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}\mathsf{Z}
and
H^{0}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})\simeq\bigoplus_{c\in\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
. Write
|\mathbf{k}|
for
\sum_{c\in\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}k_{c}
. This refined grading has the following properties, which can be proved in the same way as
[
BCGPM16
, Proposition 4.28]
:

(1)

If
\mathsf{V}_{\mathbf{k}}({\mathcal{S}})\neq 0
, then
|\mathbf{k}|=0
.

(2)

If
\mathsf{V}_{(\mathbf{k},k_{\infty})}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})\neq 0
, then
k_{\infty}=k
.

For
\mathbf{k}\in H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathsf{Z})
, set
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{k}}({\mathcal{S}})=\mathsf{V}_{(\mathbf{k},-|\mathbf{k}|)}({\mathcal{S}})
. Then we have

\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\mathcal{S}})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{k}\in H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathsf{Z})\\
|\mathbf{k}|=k\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{k}}({\mathcal{S}}).

In particular, if
\Sigma
is connected, then
H^{0}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
acts on
\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\mathcal{S}})
by
\mathcal{Z}(\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\varphi})v=\psi(\varphi,k)v
,
\varphi\in H^{0}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
.

Remark 1.14
.

As mentioned above,
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{C}}
is part of a once-extended TQFT
\check{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{C}}:\check{\mathsf{C}}\mathsf{ob}^{\mathsf{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\check{\mathsf{C}}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\Bbbk}
. Here
\check{\mathsf{C}}\mathsf{ob}^{\mathsf{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
is the bicategory of closed decorated
1
-manifolds, their decorated admissible
2
-cobordisms and their equivalence classes of decorated
3
-cobordisms with corners and
\check{\mathsf{C}}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\Bbbk}
is the bicategory of
\mathsf{Z}
-graded complete
\Bbbk
-linear categories with symmetric monoidal structure determined by
\gamma

[
DR22
, Theorem 6.1]
. The value of
\check{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathcal{C}}
on the
S^{1}
with cohomology class of holonomy
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is Morita equivalent to the ideal of projective objects of
\mathcal{C}_{g}

[
DR22
, Proposition 7.1]
.

1.6.
Finite dimensionality of TQFT state spaces

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
with associated TQFT
\mathcal{Z}
. In this section we prove that, under mild assumptions on
\mathcal{C}
, the state spaces of
\mathcal{Z}
are finite dimensional.

An object
V\in\mathcal{C}
is called a
direct sum
of a finite set of objects
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{C}
, denoted
V=\bigoplus_{i\in I}V_{i}
, if there exist morphisms
\iota_{i}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{i},V)
and
s_{i}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,V_{i})
which satisfy
\operatorname{Id}_{V}=\sum_{i\in I}\iota_{i}\circ s_{i}
and
s_{j}\circ\iota_{i}=\delta_{i,j}\operatorname{Id}_{V_{i}}
. When
\mathcal{C}
is additive, this notion of direct sum agrees with that of the additive structure of
\mathcal{C}
. An object
V\in\mathcal{C}
is called
indecomposable
if
V=\bigoplus_{i\in I}V_{i}
implies that there is a unique
i\in I
such that
V\simeq V_{i}
and
V_{j}=0
if
j\neq i
.

Definition 1.15
.

A modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category
\mathcal{C}
relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
is called
TQFT finite
if it has the following properties:

(F1)

For each
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, there exists a finite set
\{P_{j}\}_{j\in J_{g}}
of projective indecomposables of
\mathcal{C}_{g}
such that any projective indecomposable of
\mathcal{C}_{g}
is isomorphic to
P_{j}\otimes\sigma(k)
for some
j\in J_{g}
and
k\in\mathsf{Z}
.

(F2)

For any projective
P\in\mathcal{C}
, the vector space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma(k),P)
is finite dimensional for all
k\in\mathsf{Z}
and non-zero for only finitely many
k\in\mathsf{Z}
.

(F3)

the subcategory
{\mathcal{P}}\subset\mathcal{C}
of projectives is dominated by the set of projective indecomposables.

Since
{\mathcal{P}}\subset\mathcal{C}
is an ideal, property
(F2)
implies that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,P)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma(0),P\otimes V^{*})
is finite dimensional for all
V\in\mathcal{C}
and
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.

It is straightforward to verify that relative modular categories of modules over unrolled quantum groups of complex simple Lie algebras, as studied in
[
BCGPM16
,
DRGPM20
]
, are TQFT finite. Further examples will be given in Section
2
below.

Theorem 1.16
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a TQFT finite relative modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category. Then for any decorated surface
{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
, the vector space
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is finite dimensional.

Theorem
1.16
follows directly from Proposition
1.17
below and the definition of the state spaces of
\mathcal{Z}
, given in equation (
4
). To formulate Proposition
1.17
, let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n},\omega,\mathcal{L})\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Write
g
for the genus of
\Sigma
and
\epsilon_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
X_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
for the orientation and color of the point
p_{i}
, respectively. Fix
k\in\mathsf{Z}
. We construct decorated cobordisms
(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma_{c}^{\prime},\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k}
, and so vectors in
\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\mathcal{S}})
, as follows. Let
\eta
be a handlebody bounding
\Sigma
and
\tilde{\eta}=\eta\setminus\mathring{B}
, where
B\subset\mathring{\eta}
is a closed
3
-ball. Color the boundary component
\partial B\subset\partial\tilde{\eta}
by
(V_{g_{0}},1),(\sigma(k),1)
and
(V_{g_{0}},-1)
. When
g=0
, let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the ribbon graph consisting of a single coupon with
n+3
legs attached to the points
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
(V_{g_{0}},1),(\sigma(k),1)
and
(V_{g_{0}},-1)
.
When
g\geq 1
, choose a small
3
-ball
B^{3}\subset\eta
which contains
B
and
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and let
\Gamma
be an oriented spine of
\eta\setminus B
which is combinatorially equivalent to the following oriented trivalent graph:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{181.19293pt}{-50.8696pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

By convention, we set
e_{1}=e_{g+1}
when
g=1
. The orientations of
n
legs of the coupon are determined by the orientations
\{\epsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. There exists a unique element of
H^{1}(\tilde{\eta}\setminus\Gamma^{\prime};\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
whose restriction to
\partial\tilde{\eta}
is
\omega
on
\Sigma
and is compatible with the coloring of
\widehat{S}_{k}
. We denote this cohomology class again by
\omega
, although we stress that it depends on the fixed choice of
k
.

Finally, with the above notation, a
\mathcal{C}
-coloring of
\Gamma^{\prime}
is called
finite projective
if the following conditions hold:

(1)

The coloring is
\omega
-compatible.

(2)

The edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{g+1},f_{0},f_{1},\dots,f_{2g-3}
are colored by projective indecomposables.

(3)

For each
i=1,\dots,g
, the color of
e_{i}
is in the finite set
\{P_{j}\}_{j\in J_{\omega(m_{e_{i}})}}
.

Such a coloring
c
defines a decorated cobordism
(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma^{\prime}_{c},\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k}
.

Proposition 1.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a TQFT finite modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
. For any
{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
, the vector space
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
is finite dimensional for all
k\in\mathsf{Z}
and non-zero for only finitely many
k\in\mathsf{Z}
. Moreover,
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
is spanned by the decorated cobordisms
\{(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma^{\prime}_{c},\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k}\}_{c}
, where
c
runs over the finite set of finite projective
\mathcal{C}
-colorings of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n},\omega,\mathcal{L})
and
\tilde{\eta}
be as above. By part (
1
) of Lemma
1.13
,
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
is spanned by
\{(\tilde{\eta},T,\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}\}_{T}
, where
T\subset\tilde{\eta}
runs over all
\omega
-compatible
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graphs.

Suppose that
g=0
. Let
(\tilde{\eta},T,\omega,0)\in\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
. Isotope all non-trivial parts of
T
into a
3
-ball and put it into a coupon. The underlying graph is then
\Gamma^{\prime}
, as described before the proposition, and the coupon is colored by an element of

(5)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{I},\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}X^{\epsilon_{i}}_{i}\otimes V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(k)\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*}).

The vectors from the statement of the proposition therefore span
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
. The vector space (
5
) is isomorphic to

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{g_{0}},\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}X^{\epsilon_{i}}_{i}\otimes V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(k))\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma(-k),\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}X^{\epsilon_{i}}_{i}\otimes V_{g_{0}}\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*}).

Since
V_{g_{0}}
is projective, property
(F2)
implies that the vector space on the left hand side of this isomorphism is finite dimensional for all
k\in\mathsf{Z}
while that on the right is non-zero for only finitely many
k\in\mathsf{Z}
. This completes the proof when
g=0
.

Suppose now that
g\geq 1
. Define a cell decomposition of
\tilde{\eta}
into
3
-balls
B_{1},\dots,B_{2g-1}
and
B_{0}=B\setminus\mathring{B^{3}}
, a
3
-ball minus the interior of a smaller
3
-ball, by cutting
\tilde{\eta}
along
3g-1
disks
D_{e_{1}},\dots,D_{e_{g+1}},D_{f_{0}},D_{f_{1}},\dots,D_{f_{2g-3}}
as follows. The disk
D_{f_{0}}
is such that cutting along
D_{f_{0}}
leaves
B_{0}
which contains the points
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
(V_{g_{0}},1),(\sigma(k),1)
,
(V_{g_{0}},-1)
on its boundary. The remaining disks are the obvious bounding disks in
\tilde{\eta}
which intersect
\Gamma^{\prime}
exactly once at the edge by which they are labeled.

First, we show that
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
is spanned by
\{(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma^{\prime}_{c},\omega,0)\}_{c}
, where
c
runs over all
\omega
-compatible
\mathcal{C}
-colorings of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in which the edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{g+1},f_{0},\dots,f_{2g-3}
are colored by projective indecomposables. Let
(\tilde{\eta},T,\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k}
be any decorated cobordism. Let
D
be one of
D_{e_{1}},\dots,D_{e_{g+1}},D_{f_{0}},\dots,D_{f_{2g-3}}
. Since
V_{g_{0}}
is projective, we can entangle
T
with itself (more precisely, with the edge colored by
V_{g_{0}}
) by an isotopy to assume that
T
intersects
D
in at least one edge colored by a projective. Next, we modify
T
in a neighborhood of
D
as follows. We can assume that
T
intersects
D
transversally at
\mathcal{C}
-colored points forming a sequence
U=((U_{1},\delta_{1}),\ldots,(U_{t},\delta_{t}))
, where
U_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega(m_{e_{U_{i}}})}
and
\delta_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
is determined by the orientation of the edge
e_{U_{i}}
. Since at least one
U_{i}
is projective, the tensor product
F_{\mathcal{C}}(U)
is projective. By property
(F3)
, there exist projective indecomposables
W_{s}
and morphisms
f_{s}:F_{\mathcal{C}}(U)\to{W}_{s}
and
h_{s}:{W}_{s}\to F_{\mathcal{C}}(U)
such that
\operatorname{Id}_{F_{\mathcal{C}}(U)}=\sum_{s}h_{s}\circ f_{s}
.
This implies that a tubular neighborhood of
D
which consists of a cylinder containing
t
strands colored by the
U_{i}
is skein equivalent to

\sum_{s}\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-18.0,17.0){\mbox{\tiny$h_{s}$}}\put(-18.0,-14.0){\mbox{\tiny$f_{s}$}}\put(-10.0,0.0){\mbox{\tiny${W}_{s}$}}\,.

By applying these transformations at each disk
D
we obtain a skein equivalent element which is the sum of ribbon graphs
T^{\prime}
which meet each
D
on a unique edge colored by a projective indecomposable. Moreover, up to skein equivalence, we can replace the contents of each
B_{0},B_{1},\dots,B_{2g-1}
with a sum of the same
B_{i}
with a single coupon. For
j\geq 1
, the ball
B_{j}
has a single trivalent coupon while
B_{0}
has a coupon with one incoming leg connected to
D_{f_{0}}
and
n+3
outgoing legs connected to points labeled by
\{X^{\epsilon_{i}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
V_{g_{0}}
,
\sigma(k)
and
V^{*}_{g_{0}}
. It follows that
(\tilde{\eta},T,\omega,0)\in\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
is a linear combination of decorated cobordisms of the form
(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma_{c}^{\prime},\omega,0)
, where
c
is an
\omega
-compatible
\mathcal{C}
-coloring such that the colors of the edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{g+1},f_{0},\dots,f_{2g-3}
are projective indecomposables.

Next, we show that we can restrict to finitely many colorings of
\Gamma^{\prime}
while still preserving the spanning property. Let
c
be a
\mathcal{C}
-coloring of
\Gamma^{\prime}
as in the previous paragraph. Consider the pair of edges
(e_{2},f_{1})
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
with corresponding projective indecomposable colors
W_{e_{2}}
and
W_{f_{1}}
. Property
(F1)
implies that
W_{e_{2}}\simeq P_{j}\otimes\sigma(k^{\prime})
for some
j\in J_{\omega(m_{e_{2}})}
and
k^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Z}
. Consider an oriented
\sigma(k^{\prime})
-colored curve close to the edges
e_{2}
and
f_{1}
in
\Gamma^{\prime}
, the orientation chosen to be opposite to that of
e_{2}
. The union of this curve with
\Gamma^{\prime}
is
\sigma
-equivalent to
\Gamma^{\prime}
and, moreover, is skein equivalent to a coloring of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in which the edges
e_{2}
and
f_{1}
are colored by
W_{e_{2}}\otimes\sigma(k^{\prime})^{-1}\simeq P_{j}
and
W_{f_{1}}\otimes\sigma(k^{\prime})
, respectively. Repeating this process iteratively for each of the pairs
(e_{3},f_{3}),(e_{4},f_{5}),\dots,(e_{g-1},f_{2g-5}),(e_{g},f_{2g-3})
, it follows that the vector corresponding to
\Gamma^{\prime}_{c}
is proportional in
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
to a coloring of
\Gamma^{\prime}
where the color of each edge
e_{i}
,
i=2,\dots,g
, is in the finite set
\{P_{j}\}_{j\in J_{\omega(m_{e_{i}})}}
. Starting from this last coloring one can also put a
\sigma(k^{\prime\prime})
-colored curve near the edge
e_{1}
and all the edges
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{2g-3}
to find a skein equivalent element in which the color of
e_{1}
is in
\{P_{j}\}_{j\in J_{\omega(m_{e_{1}})}}
and the colors of the edges
e_{i}
,
i=2,\dots,g
, are unchanged.

Thus,
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
is spanned by
\{(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma^{\prime}_{c},\omega,0)\}_{c}
, where
c
runs over all finite projective
\mathcal{C}
-colorings of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. It remains to show that such colorings are finite and non-zero for only finitely many
k\in\mathsf{Z}
. Let
c
be such a coloring. We showed above that in the cobordism
(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma^{\prime}_{c},\omega,0)
the ball containing the trivalent vertex with the legs
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
and
f_{1}
can be represented by a single coupon having one incoming leg labeled with
P_{j}
, for some
j\in J_{\omega(m_{e_{1}})}
, and two outgoing legs labeled with projective indecomposables
W_{f_{1}}
and
P_{j^{\prime}}
for some
j^{\prime}\in J_{\omega(m_{e_{2}})}
. Property
(F1)
implies that
W_{f_{1}}\simeq P_{j^{\prime\prime}}\otimes\sigma(k^{\prime})
for some
j^{\prime\prime}\in J_{\omega(m_{f_{1}})}
and
k^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Z}
. Thus, colorings of this coupon are elements of

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(P_{j},W_{f_{1}}\otimes P_{j^{\prime}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(P_{j},P_{j^{\prime\prime}}\otimes\sigma(k^{\prime})\otimes P_{j^{\prime}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma(-k^{\prime}),P_{j^{\prime\prime}}\otimes P_{j^{\prime}}\otimes P_{j}^{*}),

which is finite dimensional for all
j,j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime}
and non-zero for only finitely many
j,j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime}
by property
(F2)
. Thus, there are only finitely many choices for the coloring
W_{f_{1}}
of
f_{1}
. A similar argument now shows that the ball containing the trivalent vertex with legs
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
and
e_{2}
has only finitely many colorings of
f_{2}
which correspond to non-zero cobordisms. Repeating this process for the edges
f_{3},\dots,f_{2g-2}
and
f_{0}
, we see that there are only finitely many colorings for each of these edges and all the corresponding colorings of coupons are finite dimensional. Finally, consider
B_{0}=B\setminus\mathring{B^{3}}
, which contains a single coupon with one incoming leg and
n+3
outgoing legs. We have seen that
f_{0}
must be colored by a projective indecomposable from the finite set

\{P_{j}\otimes\sigma(k_{i})\mid j\in J_{\omega(m_{f_{1}})}\text{ and }k_{i}\in\mathsf{Z}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,t\}.

Thus, the coupon in
B_{0}
is colored by an element of

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(P_{j}\otimes\sigma(k_{i}),\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}X^{\epsilon_{i}}_{i}\otimes V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(k)\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma(-k),\sigma(-k_{i})\otimes P_{j}^{*}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}X^{\epsilon_{i}}_{i}\otimes V_{g_{0}}\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*})

for some
j\in J_{\omega(m_{f_{1}})}
and
k_{i}\in\mathsf{Z}
,
i=1,\dots,t
, which is finite dimensional and non-zero for only finitely many
k\in\mathsf{Z}
by property
(F2)
. Since there are only finitely many choices of
j
and
k_{i}
, it follows that there are only finitely many
k\in\mathsf{Z}
where the coloring
\Gamma^{\prime}_{c}
corresponds to a non-zero vector in
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{k})
.
∎

Proposition 1.18
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
. For any
{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{C}}
, the vector spaces
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
and
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{0})
are isomorphic. Moreover, if
\mathcal{C}
is TQFT finite, then
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
is spanned by decorated cobordisms
\{(\eta,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{c},\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}\}_{c}
, where
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by deleting the three edges attached to
\widehat{S}_{k}

and
c
runs over the finite set of all finite projective
\mathcal{C}
-colorings of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

In
[
DR22
, Definition 5.2]
De Renzi defines decorated cobordisms

\widehat{D^{3}_{0}}:\emptyset\to\widehat{S}_{0}
and
\widehat{\bar{D}^{3}_{0}}:\widehat{S}_{0}\to\emptyset
. The cobordism
\widehat{D^{3}_{0}}
is the closed
3
-ball with an unknotted strand connecting the points
(V_{g_{0}},1)
and
(V_{g_{0}},-1)
on its boundary together with the unique compatible cohomology class. The cobordism
\widehat{\bar{D}^{3}_{0}}
is defined similarly, by reversing all orientations involved. Up to a factor of
{\mathscr{D}}^{-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V_{g_{0}})\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, the images of
\widehat{D^{3}_{0}}
and
\widehat{\bar{D}^{3}_{0}}
under
\mathsf{V}
are mutually inverse
[
DR22
, Lemma 5.2]
. It follows that
\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\sqcup\widehat{D^{3}_{0}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{0}
and
\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\sqcup\widehat{\bar{D}^{3}_{0}}:{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{0}\to{\mathcal{S}}
satisfy
{\mathscr{D}}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V_{g_{0}})^{-1}\mathsf{V}(\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\sqcup\widehat{D^{3}_{0}})\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\sqcup\widehat{\bar{D}}^{3}_{0})=\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{0}}
and thereby establish an isomorphism
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})\simeq\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{0})
.

Assume now that
\mathcal{C}
is TQFT finite. Let
(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma^{\prime}_{c},\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{0}
be a decorated cobordism, where
c
is a finite projective
\mathcal{C}
-coloring of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. By Proposition
1.17
, such cobordisms span
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\widehat{S}_{0})
. The cobordism
(\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\sqcup\widehat{\bar{D}^{3}_{0}})\circ(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma^{\prime}_{c},\omega,0)
is skein equivalent to a cobordism of the form
(\eta,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{c},\omega,0):\emptyset\to{\mathcal{S}}
, where
c
is a finite projective
\mathcal{C}
-coloring of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
. Such colorings therefore span
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
. An argument similar to that from the proof of Proposition
1.17
shows that the set of finite projective
\mathcal{C}
-colorings of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is finite. ∎

2.
Relative modular categories from
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))

2.1.
Conventions for superalgebra

Let
\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}=\{\bar{0},\bar{1}\}
be the additive group of order two. A
super vector space
is a
\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector space
V=V_{\bar{0}}\oplus V_{\bar{1}}
. The degree of a homogeneous element
v\in V
is denoted
\bar{v}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. A
morphism of super vector spaces of degree
\bar{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
is a linear map
f:V\rightarrow W
which satisfies
\overline{f(v)}=\bar{v}+\bar{d}
for each homogeneous
v\in V
. A (left)
module
over a superalgebra
A
is a super vector space
M
together with a superalgebra homomorphism
A\rightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk}(M)
of degree
\bar{0}
. We refer the reader to
[
DEF+99
, I-Supersymmetry]
for a detailed discussion of superalgebra.

2.2.
The unrolled quantum group of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)

Motivated by standard quantizations of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)

[
Kul89
,
KT91
,
Res92
,
Vir06
]
and the definition of the unrolled quantum group
U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))

[
GPMT09
,
CGPM15
]
, we define an unrolled version of the universal enveloping algebra of the complex Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
.

Fix
\hbar\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\pi\sqrt{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and set
q=e^{\hbar}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\pm 1\}
. For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, define
q^{z}=e^{\hbar z}
. Let
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
be the unital associative superalgebra over
\mathbb{C}
with generators
E
,
K
,
K^{-1}
,
G
of degree
\bar{0}
and
X
,
Y
of degree
\bar{1}
subject to the relations

KK^{-1}=K^{-1}K=1,

[K^{s},E]=[K^{s},G]=[K^{s},X]=[K^{s},Y]=0,\qquad s\in\{\pm 1\}

[E,X]=0,\qquad[E,Y]=0,\qquad[E,G]=0,

[G,X]=X,\qquad[G,Y]=-Y,

XY+YX=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}},

X^{2}=Y^{2}=0.

Note that both
E
and
K
are central in
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
. Define a counit
\epsilon
, coproduct
\Delta
and antipode
S
on
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
by

\epsilon(E)=\epsilon(G)=\epsilon(X)=\epsilon(Y)=0,\qquad\epsilon(K)=1,

\Delta(E)=E\otimes 1+1\otimes E,\qquad\Delta(G)=G\otimes 1+1\otimes G,\qquad\Delta K=K\otimes K,

\Delta(X)=X\otimes K^{-1}+1\otimes X,\qquad\Delta(Y)=Y\otimes 1+K\otimes Y,

S(E)=-E,\qquad S(G)=-G,\qquad S(K)=K^{-1},\qquad S(X)=-XK,\qquad S(Y)=-YK^{-1}

and requiring
\epsilon
and
\Delta
to be superalgebra homomorphisms and
S
to be a superalgebra anti-homomorphism. This gives
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
the structure of a Hopf superalgebra. The subalgebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))\subset U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
generated by
K^{\pm 1}
,
G
,
X
,
Y
is a standard quantization of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
.

2.3.
Weight modules

In this section we describe the category of weight modules over
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
. See
[
Vir06
, §11]
,
[
Sar15
, §3]
for similar descriptions of module categories for standard quantizations of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
.

2.3.1.
The category of weight modules

Let
V
be a
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module. A homogeneous vector
v\in V
is called a
weight vector
if it is a simultaneous eigenvector of
E
and
G
, say,
Ev=\lambda_{E}v
and
Gv=\lambda_{G}v
, in which case
\lambda=(\lambda_{E},\lambda_{G})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the
weight
of
v
. A finite dimensional
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module
V
is called a
weight module
if it is a direct sum of weight spaces and
Kv=q^{\lambda_{E}}v
for any weight vector
v
of weight
\lambda
. Since the action of
K
on a weight module can be deduced from that of
E
, we often omit it. Let
\mathcal{D}^{q}
be the abelian category of weight
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-modules and their
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-linear maps of degree
\bar{0}
. Unless mentioned otherwise, all
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-modules considered in this paper are assumed to be weight modules.

Given
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
, let
V^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
be the
\mathbb{C}
-linear dual of
V
with
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module structure

(x\cdot f)(v)=(-1)^{\bar{f}\bar{x}}f(S(x)v),\qquad v\in V,\;f\in V^{*},\;x\in U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)).

Let
\{v_{i}\}_{i}
be a homogeneous basis of
V
with dual basis
\{v_{i}^{*}\}_{i}
. Define

(6)

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}(f\otimes v)=f(v),\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}(1)=\sum_{i}v_{i}\otimes v_{i}^{*}

and

(7)

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}(v\otimes f)=(-1)^{\bar{f}\bar{v}}f(Kv),\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V}(1)=\sum_{i}(-1)^{\bar{v}_{i}}v_{i}^{*}\otimes K^{-1}v_{i}.

Lemma 2.1
.

The maps (
6
) and (
7
) define a pivotal structure on
\mathcal{D}^{q}
.

Proof.

That the maps (
6
) and (
7
) are
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-linear and satisfy the snake relations is a straightforward calculation. For pivotality, we need to verify that for each morphism
f:V\rightarrow W
in
\mathcal{D}^{q}
, its left and right duals
W^{*}\rightarrow V^{*}
are equal and that the canonical isomorphisms
(V\otimes W)^{*}\rightarrow W^{*}\otimes V^{*}
constructed using left and right dualities are equal. See
[
EGNO15
, §2.10]
. In both cases, the morphisms constructed from left duality are the standard super vector space such morphisms. That right duality gives the same morphisms follows from the fact that
K
appears in
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}
while
K^{-1}
appears in
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}
.
∎

It follows that the quantum dimension of
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
is
\operatorname{qdim}V=\sum_{i}(-1)^{\bar{v}_{i}}v_{i}^{*}(Kv_{i})
.

Let
V,W\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
. Define
\Upsilon_{V,W}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V\otimes W)
by

\Upsilon_{V,W}(v\otimes w)=q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}v\otimes w,

where
v\in V
and
w\in W
are of weight
\lambda
and
\mu
, respectively. Define
\tilde{R}_{V,W}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V\otimes W)
to be left multiplication by
1+(q-q^{-1})(X\otimes Y)(K\otimes K^{-1})
and
c_{V,W}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V\otimes W,W\otimes V)
by

c_{V,W}=\tau_{V,W}\circ\tilde{R}_{V,W}\circ\Upsilon_{V,W},

where
\tau_{V,W}:V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V
,
v\otimes w\mapsto(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}}w\otimes v
, is the standard symmetric braiding on the category of super vector spaces.

Proposition 2.2
.

The maps
\{c_{V,W}:V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V\}_{V,W\in\mathcal{D}^{q}}
define a braiding on
\mathcal{D}^{q}
.

Proof.

That
c_{V,W}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism follows from the corresponding statements for
\tau_{V,W}
and
\Upsilon_{V,W}
and the observation that the inverse of
\tilde{R}_{V,W}
is left multiplication by
1-(q-q^{-1})(X\otimes Y)(K\otimes K^{-1})
. To verify that
c_{V,W}
is
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-linear, we verify that it commutes with the action of the generators of
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
. We give the calculation only for the generator
X
. Fix
v\in V
of weight
\lambda
and
w\in W
of weight
\mu
. We compute

c_{V,W}(X\cdot v\otimes w)=(-1)^{(\bar{v}+\bar{1})\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-(\lambda_{G}+2)\mu_{E}}w\otimes Xv+(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}(\mu_{G}+1)-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}Xw\otimes v\\
+(-1)^{\bar{v}(\bar{w}+\bar{1})}(q-q^{-1})q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-(\lambda_{G}+1)\mu_{E}}YXw\otimes Xv

and

c_{V,W}(v\otimes w)=(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}w\otimes v+(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}+\bar{w}+\bar{1}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}(q-q^{-1})q^{\lambda_{E}-\mu_{E}}Yw\otimes Xv

so that

\displaystyle X\cdot c_{V,W}(v\otimes w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}-\lambda_{E}}Xw\otimes v+(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}+\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}w\otimes Xv+

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}+\bar{w}+\bar{1}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}(q-q^{-1})q^{\lambda_{E}-\mu_{E}-\lambda_{E}}XYw\otimes Xv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}(\mu_{G}+1)-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}Xw\otimes v+(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}+\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}w\otimes Xv+

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}+\bar{w}+\bar{1}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-(\lambda_{G}+1)\mu_{E}}(q-q^{-1})(-YX+\frac{q^{\mu_{E}}-q^{-\mu_{E}}}{q-q^{-1}})w\otimes Xv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}(\mu_{G}+1)-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}Xw\otimes v+(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}+\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}w\otimes Xv+

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}+\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-(\lambda_{G}+1)\mu_{E}}(q-q^{-1})YXw\otimes Xv+

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{v}\bar{w}+\bar{w}+\bar{1}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-(\lambda_{G}+1)\mu_{E}}(q^{\mu_{E}}-q^{-\mu_{E}})w\otimes Xv,

which is equal to
c_{V,W}(X\cdot v\otimes w)
.

Next, consider the hexagon identities, in which we suppress all associators. Let
U,V,W\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
. We verify that
c_{U,V\otimes W}=(\operatorname{Id}_{V}\otimes c_{U,W})\circ(c_{U,V}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{W})
; verification of the second hexagon identity is analogous. Fix vectors
u\in U
,
v\in V
and
w\in W
of weights
\lambda
,
\mu
and
\nu
, respectively. We compute

\displaystyle c_{U,V\otimes W}(u\otimes v\otimes w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{(\bar{v}+\bar{w})\bar{u}}q^{-\lambda_{E}(\mu_{G}+\nu_{G})-\lambda_{G}(\mu_{E}+\nu_{E})}v\otimes w\otimes u+(-1)^{\bar{u}+(\bar{u}+\bar{1})(\bar{v}+\bar{1}+\bar{w})}\cdot

\displaystyle q^{-\lambda_{E}(\mu_{G}+\nu_{G})-\lambda_{G}(\mu_{E}+\nu_{E})}(q-q^{-1})q^{\lambda_{E}-\mu_{E}-\nu_{E}}Yv\otimes w\otimes Xu

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\bar{u}+\bar{v}+(\bar{u}+\bar{1})(\bar{v}+\bar{w}+1)}q^{-\lambda_{E}(\mu_{G}+\nu_{G})-\lambda_{G}(\mu_{E}+\nu_{E})}(q-q^{-1})\cdot

\displaystyle q^{\lambda_{E}-\nu_{E}}v\otimes Yw\otimes Xu.

The image of
u\otimes v\otimes w
under
(\operatorname{Id}_{V}\otimes c_{U,W})\circ(c_{U,V}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{W})
is

\displaystyle u\otimes v\otimes w

\displaystyle\xmapsto{c_{U,V}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{u}+(\bar{u}+\bar{1})(\bar{v}+\bar{1})}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}q^{\lambda_{E}-\mu_{E}}(q-q^{-1})Yv\otimes Xu\otimes w

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\operatorname{Id}\otimes c_{U,W}}

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{u}\bar{v}}(-1)^{\bar{u}\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\nu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\nu_{E}}v\otimes w\otimes u

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\bar{u}\bar{v}}(-1)^{(\bar{u}+\bar{1})(\bar{w}+\bar{1})}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\nu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\nu_{E}}q^{\lambda_{E}-\nu_{E}}(q-q^{-1})\cdot
