\displaystyle q^{-\lambda_{E}\nu_{G}-(\lambda_{G}+1)\nu_{E}}(-1)^{\bar{u}+\bar{v}}(-1)^{\bar{v}}v\otimes Yw\otimes Xu

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\bar{u}}(-1)^{(\bar{u}+\bar{1})(\bar{v}+\bar{1})}(-1)^{\bar{v}+\bar{1})\bar{w}}q^{-\lambda_{E}\mu_{G}-\lambda_{G}\mu_{E}}q^{-\lambda_{E}-\mu_{E}}(q-q^{-1})\cdot

\displaystyle q^{-\lambda_{E}\nu_{G}-(\lambda_{G}+1)\nu_{E}}Yv\otimes w\otimes Xu.

Direct comparison shows that this expression agrees with
c_{U,V\otimes W}(u\otimes v\otimes w)
.
∎

2.3.2.
Simple modules

Given
z\in\mathbb{C}
, put
[z]_{q}=\frac{q^{z}-q^{-z}}{q-q^{-1}}
. Note that
[z]_{q}=0
if and only if
z\in\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
.

Given
(n,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}
and
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
, let
\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
be the one dimensional
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module with basis vector
v
of degree
\overline{p}
and module structure

Ev=\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}v,\qquad Gv=bv,\qquad Xv=0,\qquad Yv=0.

Following
[
Vir06
, §11.4]
, the
quantum Kac module
of weight
(\alpha,a)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
and degree
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
is the
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
with basis vectors
v
of degree
\bar{p}
and
v^{\prime}
of degree
\bar{p}+\bar{1}
and

Ev=\alpha v,\qquad Gv=av,\qquad Xv=0,\qquad Yv=v^{\prime},

Ev^{\prime}=\alpha v^{\prime},\qquad Gv^{\prime}=(a-1)v^{\prime},\qquad Yv^{\prime}=0,\qquad Xv^{\prime}=[\alpha]_{q}v.

The structure of
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is illustrated by the diagram

V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}:\qquad\hbox to113.22pt{\vbox to56.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.60844pt\lower-28.08357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-53.27544pt}{-24.75056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}.base}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

In this diagram, and those which follow, the actions of
G
and
E
are depicted as loops above and below the weight vectors, respectively, and an arrow labeled by a generator indicates that it acts by the identity in the given basis. The module
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is simple if and only if
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, in which case the dual vector
v^{\prime*}\in V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}^{*}
is highest weight and

(8)

V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}^{*}\simeq V(-\alpha,-(a-1))_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}.

If instead
\alpha=\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is indecomposable and fits in the exact sequence

(9)

0\rightarrow\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a-1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}\rightarrow V(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{p}}\rightarrow\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{p}}\rightarrow 0.

Proposition 2.3
.

Every simple object of
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is isomorphic to exactly one of the modules on the list

•

\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
with
(n,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}
and
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
,

•

V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
with
(\alpha,a)\in(\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{C}
and
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
be simple. Since
X^{2}=0
, there exists a highest weight vector
v\in V
, say of weight
\lambda(\lambda_{E},\lambda_{G})
, and
V=U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))\cdot v
. If
Yv=0
, then
(K-K^{-1})v=0
, which implies
\lambda_{E}\in\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
and
V\simeq\varepsilon(\lambda_{E},\lambda_{G})_{\bar{v}}
. If instead
Yv\neq 0
, then
V\simeq V(\lambda_{E},\lambda_{G})_{\bar{v}}
and, by simplicity and the discussion above the proposition,
\lambda_{E}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is locally finite.

Proof.

That morphism spaces in
\mathcal{D}^{q}
are finite dimensional is clear. To see that
\mathcal{D}^{q}
has finite length, let
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
be non-zero. There exists a highest weight vector
v\in V
which generates a submodule
\langle v\rangle
of the form
\varepsilon(\lambda_{E},\lambda_{G})_{\bar{v}}
with
\lambda_{E}\in\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
or
V(\lambda_{E},\lambda_{G})_{\bar{v}}
with
\lambda_{E}\in\mathbb{C}
. Using the filtration (
9
) of
V(\lambda_{E},\lambda_{G})_{\bar{v}}
if
\lambda_{E}\in\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
and simplicity of
\langle v\rangle
otherwise, the problem of finding a Jordan–Hölder filtration of
V
reduces to that of
V/\penalty\langle v\rangle
, whose dimension strictly less than that of
V
. Iterating this argument completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.5
.

If
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, then
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
is projective and injective.

Proof.

It follows easily from Lemma
2.4
that
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is multitensor, whence projective objects are injective
[
EGNO15
, Proposition 6.1.3]
. It therefore suffices to prove that
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is projective. There is a
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module isomorphism

V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\simeq U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))\otimes_{U_{q}^{E,\geq 0}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))}\mathbb{C}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}},

where
U_{q}^{E,\geq 0}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
is the subalgebra of
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
generated by
E
,
G
,
K^{\pm 1}
and
X
and
\mathbb{C}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is the one dimensional weight
U_{q}^{E,\geq 0}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module concentrated in degree
\bar{p}
of weight
(\alpha,a)
and on which
X
acts by zero. Let
f:V\rightarrow W
be an epimorphism in
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\phi:V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\rightarrow W
a non-zero morphism. Since

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}},W)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\geq 0}}(\mathbb{C}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}},W_{|U_{q}^{E,\geq 0}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))}),

where
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\geq 0}
denotes the category of weight
U_{q}^{E,\geq 0}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-modules, the map
\phi
is determined by a morphism
\mathbb{C}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\rightarrow W_{|U_{q}^{E,\geq 0}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))}
in
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\geq 0}
, that is, a highest weight vector
w\in W
of weight
(\alpha,a)
and degree
\bar{p}
. By assumption,
w
has a weight vector preimage in
V_{\bar{p}}
, say
v
. Since
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, we may form
v^{\prime}=v-[\alpha]_{q}^{-1}FEv\in V
, which is highest weight of weight
(\alpha,a)
and degree
\bar{p}
. The assignment
1\mapsto v^{\prime}
defines a morphism
\mathbb{C}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\rightarrow V_{|U_{q}^{E,\geq 0}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))}
in
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\geq 0}
which in turn determines a morphism
\tilde{\phi}:V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\rightarrow V
in
\mathcal{D}^{q}
, by the argument above. Since
f(v^{\prime})=w
, the map
\tilde{\phi}
satisfies
f\circ\tilde{\phi}=\phi
. Hence,
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is projective.
∎

For later use, define the
quantum anti-Kac module
of lowest weight
(\alpha,a)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
and degree
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
to be the
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module
\overline{V}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
with basis vectors
v^{\prime}
of degree
\bar{p}
and
v
of degree
\bar{p}+\bar{1}
and module structure

\overline{V}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}:\qquad\hbox to110.44pt{\vbox to56.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 55.21954pt\lower-28.35973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-51.88654pt}{-25.02672pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}.base}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The module
\overline{V}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is simple if and only if
\alpha\notin\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, in which case
\overline{V}(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\simeq V(\alpha,a+1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}
. If
\alpha=\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
\overline{V}(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{p}}
is indecomposable and fits in the exact sequence

0\rightarrow\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a+1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}\rightarrow\overline{V}(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{p}}\rightarrow\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{p}}\rightarrow 0.

Finally, we record that

(10)

\operatorname{qdim}\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}=(-1)^{\bar{p}+n}

and
\operatorname{qdim}V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}=0
.

2.3.3.
Projective indecomposable modules

Given
(n,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}
and
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
, let
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
be the
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module with basis
\{v,v^{\prime},v_{+},v_{-}\}
, where
v,v^{\prime}
are of degree
\bar{p}
and
v_{+},v_{-}
are of degree
\bar{p}+\bar{1}
, and module structure

P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}:\qquad\hbox to141.67pt{\vbox to99.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.83638pt\lower-49.97884pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-67.50337pt}{-46.64583pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}.base}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Here
E
acts everywhere by
\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}
and is omitted from the diagram. Setting
M_{1}=\operatorname{span}\{v\}
and
M_{2}=\operatorname{span}\{v,v_{+},v_{-}\}
defines a filtration
0\subset M_{1}\subset M_{2}\subset M_{3}=P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
with

M_{1}\simeq M_{3}/\penalty M_{2}\simeq\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}

and

M_{2}/\penalty M_{1}\simeq\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b+1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}\oplus\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b-1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}.

In this paragraph, fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and write
V(b)_{\bar{p}}
for
V(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
, and similarly for
P(b)_{\bar{p}}
and
\varepsilon(b)_{\bar{p}}
. If a basis vector is sent to zero under a map, then we omit it from the notation. There are morphisms

(11)

P(b)_{\bar{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\varepsilon(b)_{\bar{p}},\qquad v^{\prime}\mapsto v

and
\varepsilon(b)_{\bar{p}}\hookrightarrow P(b)_{\bar{p}}
,
v\mapsto v
. The composition

x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b,\bar{p}}:P(b)_{\bar{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\varepsilon(b)_{\bar{p}}\hookrightarrow P(b)_{\bar{p}},\qquad v^{\prime}\mapsto v

is nilpotent of order two. There are canonical projections

P(b)_{\bar{p}}\twoheadrightarrow V(b)_{\bar{p}},\qquad v^{\prime}\mapsto v,\;\;v_{-}\mapsto v^{\prime}

and

P(b)_{\bar{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{V}(b)_{\bar{p}},\qquad v^{\prime}\mapsto v^{\prime},\;\;v_{+}\mapsto v.

There are canonical inclusions

V(b+1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}\hookrightarrow P(b)_{\bar{p}}\qquad v^{\prime}\mapsto v,\;\;v\mapsto-v_{+}

and

\overline{V}(b-1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}\hookrightarrow P(b)_{\bar{p}},\qquad v^{\prime}\mapsto v_{-},\;\;v\mapsto v.

Consider the compositions

a_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b,\bar{p}}^{-}:P(b)_{\bar{p}}\twoheadrightarrow V(b)_{\bar{p}}\hookrightarrow P(b-1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}},\qquad v^{\prime}\mapsto-v_{+},\;\;v_{-}\mapsto v

and

a_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b,\bar{p}}^{+}:P(b)_{\bar{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{V}(b)_{\bar{p}}\hookrightarrow P(b+1)_{\bar{p}+1},\qquad v^{\prime}\mapsto v_{-},\;\;v_{+}\mapsto v.

Proposition 2.6
.

If
n_{1}\neq n_{2}
, then

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(P(\frac{n_{1}\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{1})_{\bar{p}_{1}},P(\frac{n_{2}\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{2})_{\bar{p}_{2}})=0.

Otherwise, there are isomorphisms

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{1})_{\bar{p}_{1}},P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{2})_{\bar{p}_{2}})\simeq\\
\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}[x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{1},\bar{p}_{1}}]/\penalty\langle x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{1},\bar{p}_{1}}^{2}\rangle&\mbox{if }(b_{2},\bar{p}_{2})=(b_{1},\bar{p}_{1}),\\
\mathbb{C}\cdot a_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{1},\bar{p}_{1}}^{\pm}&\mbox{if }(b_{2},\bar{p}_{2})=(b_{1}\pm 1,\bar{p}_{1}+\bar{1}),\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Moreover, the relations
a_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b\mp 1,\bar{p}+1}^{\pm}\circ a_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b,\bar{p}}^{\mp}=\mp x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b,\bar{p}}
hold.

Proof.

That
x
and
a^{\pm}
are the only morphisms follows by analyzing the filtration
M_{\bullet}
of
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
given above. The remaining statements are clear.
∎

2.3.4.
Tensor products

By comparing weights, it is easy to verify the isomorphisms

(12)

\varepsilon(\frac{n_{1}\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{1})_{\bar{p}_{1}}\otimes\varepsilon(\frac{n_{2}\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{2})_{\bar{p}_{2}}\simeq\varepsilon(\frac{(n_{1}+n_{2})\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b_{1}+b_{2})_{\bar{p}_{1}+\bar{p}_{2}},

(13)

V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\otimes\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{q}}\simeq V(\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a+b)_{\bar{p}+\bar{q}}

and

P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}\simeq P(0,b)_{\bar{0}}\otimes\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{p}}.

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
. If
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, then an argument using weight vectors and the injectivity of Proposition
2.5
gives

(14)

V(\alpha_{1},a_{1})_{\bar{p}_{1}}\otimes V(\alpha_{2},a_{2})_{\bar{p}_{2}}\simeq V(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},a_{1}+a_{2})_{\bar{p}_{1}+\bar{p}_{2}}\oplus V(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},a_{1}+a_{2}-1)_{\bar{p}_{1}+\bar{p}_{2}+\bar{1}}.

See
[
Vir06
, §11.6.B]
for an explicit isomorphism. If instead
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then there is an isomorphism

(15)

I:V(\alpha_{1},a_{1})_{\bar{p}_{1}}\otimes V(\alpha_{2},a_{2})_{\bar{p}_{2}}\xrightarrow{\sim}P(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},a_{1}+a_{2}-1)_{\bar{p}_{1}+\bar{p}_{2}+\bar{1}}.

See
[
Sar15
, §3]
. Explicitly, if
v_{i}\in V(\alpha_{i},a_{i})_{\bar{p}_{i}}
is highest weight and
v_{i}^{\prime}=Yv_{i}
, then

I
is defined by

I(X(v^{\prime}_{1}\otimes v^{\prime}_{2}))=v^{\prime},\qquad I(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=v_{+},

I(v_{1}^{\prime}\otimes v_{2}^{\prime})=v_{-},\qquad I(v_{1}^{\prime}\otimes v_{2}^{\prime})=v.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
(n,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}
and
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. The module
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
is projective indecomposable and the morphism (
11
) is the projective cover of
\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.6
, the only idempotent endomorphisms of
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
are multiples of the identity. Hence,
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
is indecomposable. For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, the isomorphism (
15
) gives

V(\alpha,b+1)_{\bar{1}}\otimes V(-\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}\simeq P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}.

Since
V(\alpha,b+1)_{\bar{1}}
is projective by Proposition
2.5
, so too is
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
. This proves the first statement. The second statement follows from the fact that
v^{\prime}
generates
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{q}
has enough projectives.

Proof.

Let
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
. For each
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, there is a surjection

V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}^{*}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{V}}\mathbb{C}\otimes V\simeq V.

Since
V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}
is projective by Proposition
2.5
, so too is
V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}^{*}\otimes V
.
∎

2.4.
Modified traces

We establish the existence of a modified trace on the ideal
{\mathcal{P}}
of projectives of
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and make some computations required for later use.

The following result can also be proved using the notion of ambidextrous objects introduced in
[
GPMT09
,
GKPM11
]
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Up to a global scalar, there exists a unique m-trace
\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}
on the ideal
{\mathcal{P}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q}
of projective objects.

Proof.

By Lemmas
2.4
and
2.8
,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is a locally finite pivotal
\mathbb{C}
-linear tensor category with enough projectives. It follows that
{\mathcal{P}}
has a unique non-trivial right m-trace if and only if the projective cover of the trivial module
\mathbb{C}\simeq\varepsilon(0,0)_{\bar{0}}
is self-dual
[
GKPM22
, Corollary 5.6]
. By Lemma
2.7
, the projective cover in question is
P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}
. For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, the isomorphisms (
8
) and (
15
) give
P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}\simeq V(\alpha,1)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha,1)_{\bar{0}}^{*}
, establishing self-duality. By Proposition
2.2
,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is braided. Hence, this right m-trace is an m-trace.
∎

Note that
[1]_{q}=1
for all
q\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\pm 1\}
. In particular, the module
V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}
is projective. Fix the normalization of the m-trace of Proposition
2.9
by requiring
\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(1,0)_{\bar{0}})=(q-q^{-1})^{-1}
.

Given
V,V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
, define

\Phi_{V,V^{\prime}}=(\operatorname{Id}_{V^{\prime}}\otimes\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V})\circ(c_{V,{V^{\prime}}}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{V^{*}})\circ(c_{{V^{\prime}},V}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{V^{*}})\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{V^{\prime}}\otimes\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V})\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({V^{\prime}}).

Extend the definition of
\Phi_{V,V^{\prime}}
to formal
\mathbb{C}
-linear combinations of objects of
\mathcal{D}^{q}
by bilinearity. When
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V^{\prime})\simeq\mathbb{C}
set

S^{\prime}(V,V^{\prime})=\left<\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\right>\put(-15.0,10.0){\mbox{\tiny$V$}}\put(-36.0,18.0){\mbox{\tiny$V^{\prime}$}}=\left<\Phi_{V,{V^{\prime}}}\right>\in\mathbb{C}.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,a,b\in\mathbb{C}
and
\bar{p},\bar{s}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. The following equalities hold:

\Phi_{V(\beta,b)_{\bar{p}},V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}}=(-1)^{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}q^{-2(\alpha b+a\beta)}q^{\alpha+\beta}(q^{\alpha}-q^{-\alpha})\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}}

\Phi_{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}},V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}}=(-1)^{\bar{p}+1}q^{-2(\alpha b+a\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar})}(q^{\alpha}-q^{-\alpha})^{2}\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}}

\Phi_{V(\beta,b)_{\bar{p}},P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{s}}}=(-1)^{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}q^{-2(a\beta+b\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar})}(q-q^{-1})(q^{\beta}-q^{-\beta})x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a,\bar{s}}.

Proof.

Using that
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}
is a highest weight module, it is straightforward to verify that
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}})\simeq\mathbb{C}
. It follows that
\Phi_{V,V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}}
maps a highest weight vector of
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}
to a multiple of itself, whence only the diagonal part of the braiding contributes to
\Phi_{V,V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}}
. Using this observation, the first two equalities are direct calculations. Turning to the final equality, Proposition
2.6
implies that

\Phi_{V(\beta,b)_{\bar{p}},P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{s}}}=c_{1}\operatorname{Id}_{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{s}}}+c_{2}x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a,\bar{s}}

for unique
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
. Since
\Phi_{V(\beta,b)_{\bar{p}},P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{s}}}
annihilates the highest weight vector
v_{+}
, we have
c_{1}=0
. A direct calculation shows that

\Phi_{V(\beta,b)_{\bar{p}},P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{s}}}(v^{\prime})=(-1)^{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}q^{-2(a\beta+a\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar})}(q-q^{-1})(q^{\beta}-q^{-\beta})v=c_{2}v,

from which the lemma follows.
∎

In preparation for the next result, we compute some modified dimensions. Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
. Cyclicity of the m-trace implies
\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}}(\Phi_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}},V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}}(\Phi_{V(1,0)_{\bar{0}},V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}})
. Using Lemma
2.10
to compute both sides of this equality gives

(16)

\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}})=(-1)^{\bar{p}}(q^{\alpha}-q^{-\alpha})^{-1}.

The isomorphism (
8
) and equation (
16
) imply

(17)

\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}^{*})=\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}).

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
(n,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}
and
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}})=0
and

\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}}(x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b,\bar{p}})=(-1)^{\bar{p}}(q-q^{-1})^{-1}.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
. The isomorphism (
15
) gives

V(\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}\otimes V(-\alpha,1)_{\bar{1}}\simeq P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}.

The partial trace property of the m-trace gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V(\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}\otimes V(-\alpha,1)_{\bar{1}}}(\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}\otimes V(-\alpha,1)_{\bar{1}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V(\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}}(\operatorname{ptr}_{V(-\alpha,1)_{\bar{1}}}(\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{V(-\alpha,1)_{\bar{1}}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V(\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}}(\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha+\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}})\operatorname{qdim}V(-\alpha,1)_{\bar{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

since
\operatorname{qdim}V(-\alpha,1)_{\bar{1}}=0
. Turning to the second equality, cyclicity of the m-trace implies

\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}}(\Phi_{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}},V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}}}(\Phi_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{s}},{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}}}).

Taking the m-trace of the second and third equalities of Lemma
2.10
, equating them and solving for
\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a)_{\bar{p}}}(x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},a,\bar{p}})
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
and
I
the isomorphism (
15
). The algebra isomorphism

I^{*}:\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}^{*})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}),\qquad f\mapsto I\circ f\circ I^{-1}

maps
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}}
to
(q-q^{-1})\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}})\cdot x_{0,0,\bar{0}}
.

Proof.

Write
V
for
V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}
. By Proposition
2.6
, there exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

(18)

I^{*}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V})=c_{1}\operatorname{Id}_{P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}+c_{2}x_{0,0,\bar{0}}.

Since
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}
is nilpotent,
c_{1}=0
. Taking the m-trace of the left hand side of equation (
18
) gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}(I^{*}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V\otimes V^{*}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V}\left(\operatorname{ptr}_{V^{*}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V}(\operatorname{Id}_{V})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V).

The first and second equalities follow from the cyclicity and partial trace properties of the m-trace, respectively, and the third from the snake axioms of left and right dualities after using the canonical identifications
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V^{*}}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V^{*}}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}
. On the other hand, Lemma
2.11
shows that the m-trace of the right hand side of equation (
18
) is
c_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}(x_{0,0,\bar{0}})=c_{2}(q-q^{-1})^{-1}
. It follows that
c_{2}=(q-q^{-1})\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}})
.
∎

2.5.
Generic semisimplicity and the ribbon structure of
\mathcal{D}^{q}

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}
. For each
\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, denote by
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q}
the full subcategory of modules on which
E
acts by
\alpha
.

Proposition 2.13
.

The
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-graded category
\mathcal{D}^{q}\simeq\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}}\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}
is generically semisimple with small symmetric subset
\mathsf{X}=\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, if
\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, then
\{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\mid a\in\mathbb{C},\,\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\}
is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

That
\mathcal{D}^{q}\simeq\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}}\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}
is a
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-grading follows from the definition of the coproduct and antipode of
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
. Let
\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
and
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}
non-zero. As in the proof of Proposition
2.3
, there exists a highest weight vector
v\in V
. Since
\alpha\notin\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, this vector generates a submodule
\langle v\rangle\subset V
isomorphic to
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
which, by Proposition
2.5
, is simple and injective. It follows that there exists a splitting
V\simeq V^{\prime}\oplus\langle v\rangle
. Iterating this process shows that
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}
is semisimple with the claimed completely reduced dominating set.
∎

Let
\theta:\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}\Rightarrow\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
be the natural automorphism with components
\theta_{V}=\operatorname{ptr}_{R}(c_{V,V})
,
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
.

Theorem 2.14
.

The natural automorphism
\theta
gives
\mathcal{D}^{q}
the structure of a
\mathbb{C}
-linear ribbon category.

Proof.

We have already seen in Lemma
2.1
that
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is pivotal and in Proposition
2.2
that
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is braided. It is automatic that
\theta
satisfies the balancing conditions. Let
(\alpha,a)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. We claim that
\theta_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}}=q^{-2\alpha a+\alpha}\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}}
. Since
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}})\simeq\mathbb{C}
, it suffices to compute
\theta_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}}v
for a highest weight vector
v
:

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}}}

\displaystyle v\otimes v\otimes v^{*}+v\otimes v^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime*}

\displaystyle\xmapsto{c_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}},V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}}}

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bar{p}}q^{-2\alpha a}v\otimes v\otimes v^{*}+(-1)^{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}q^{-2\alpha a+\alpha}v^{\prime}\otimes v\otimes v^{\prime*}

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}}}

\displaystyle q^{-2\alpha a+\alpha}v.

View
\mathcal{D}^{q}
as generically semisimple as in Proposition
2.13
. Using the classification of simples from Proposition
2.3
and the isomorphism (
8
), we conclude that
\theta_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}^{*}}=\theta_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}}^{*}
for all generic simples, that is, when
\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
. The assumptions of
[
GPM10
, Theorem 9]
are therefore satisfied and we conclude that
\theta
is a compatible twist.
∎

Remark 2.15
.

Variants of Proposition
2.2
and Theorem
2.14
are known:

(1)

Following Kulish
[
Kul89
]
and Khoroshkin and Tolstoy
[
KT91
, §7]
, Viro explained that
U_{h}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
, the
h
-adic quantum group of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
, admits a ribbon structure. In particular, the category of topological
U_{h}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-modules is ribbon
[
Vir06
, §11.3]
.

(2)

Sartori proved that the category of finite dimensional weight modules over
U_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
, the quantum group of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
over
\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{q})
with
\mathbf{q}
an indeterminant, is ribbon by transferring the ribbon structure of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))

[
Sar15
, §4.1]
, following the method of Tanisaki
[
Tan92
, §4]
.

Our proof of Proposition
2.2
is similar in spirit to that of Sartori
[
Sar15
]
, in that the definition of the ribbon structure of
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is motivated by the form of the universal
R
-matrix of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
. Our proof of Theorem
2.14
is however different, leveraging the generic semisimplicity of
\mathcal{D}^{q}
.

2.6.
Relative modularity of
\mathcal{D}^{q}
for arbitrary
q

Continue to denote
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}
and
\mathsf{X}=\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
and view
\mathcal{D}^{q}
as generically semisimple as in Proposition
2.13
. Let
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\{\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{p}}\}_{(b,\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}
is a free realisation of
\mathsf{Z}
in
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{0}
. Indeed, it is immediate that
\theta_{\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{p}}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{p}}}
. The remaining parts of Definition
1.5
follow from equation (
10
), the isomorphisms (
12
) and (
13
) and Proposition
2.3
. For each
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}
, a direct computation gives

c_{V,\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{s}}}\circ c_{\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{s}},V}=q^{-2\alpha b}\operatorname{Id}_{\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{s}}\otimes V}.

Thus,
\psi:\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
(\alpha,(b,\bar{p}))\mapsto q^{-2\alpha b}
, is a bicharacter satisfying equation (
1
).

Theorem 2.16
.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is a modular
\mathbb{C}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z},\mathsf{X}=\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z})
with relative modularity parameter
\zeta=-1
. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is TQFT finite.

Proof.

As explained above,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-graded and
\{\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{p}}\}_{(b,\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}
is a free realisation of
\mathsf{Z}
in
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{0}
satisfying the compatibility condition of Definition
1.9
. Proposition
2.9
shows that the full subcategory of projectives
{\mathcal{P}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q}
has an m-trace. Define
\Theta(\alpha)=\{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}\}
,
\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
. The isomorphisms (
13
) show that
\Theta(\alpha)\otimes\sigma(\mathsf{Z})=\{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\mid a\in\mathbb{C},\;\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\}
which, by Proposition
2.13
, is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}
. Thus,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is a pre-modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Z})
.

Next, we find a relative modularity parameter
\zeta
. Let
\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
. Up to isomorphism and the action of
\mathsf{Z}
, each of the categories
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{\beta}
have a unique simple object, namely
V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}
and
V(\beta,0)_{\bar{0}}
, respectively. Let
f
be the morphism corresponding to the left hand side of equation (
2
) with
\Omega_{h}=\Omega_{\beta}
and
V_{i}=V_{j}=V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}
and
\tilde{I}:V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}^{*}\to P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}
the isomorphism obtained by combining the isomorphisms (
8
) and (
15
). We compute

\displaystyle\tilde{I}^{*}f

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}})\Phi_{\Omega_{\beta},P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}})\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta,0)_{\bar{0}})\Phi_{V(\beta,0)_{\bar{0}},P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}})(q-q^{-1})x_{0,0,\bar{0}}.

The third equality follows from Lemma
2.10
. Lemma
2.12
then gives
f=-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}}
, whence
\zeta=-1
.

Turning to TQFT finiteness, in the notation of Definition
1.15
we have
J_{\alpha}=\{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}\}
if
\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
and
J_{\alpha}=\{P(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}\}
if
\alpha\in\mathsf{X}
, whence property
(F1)
holds. Since
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is locally finite abelian (see Lemma
2.4
), it is Krull–Schmidt. Properties
(F2)
-
(F3)
follow easily from this and the classification of projective indecomposables from Section
2.3
.
∎

Let
\alpha\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
. The left hand side of the diagram in Definition
1.10
which defines
\Delta_{-}
is equal to

\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}})q^{-\alpha}q^{-\alpha}\Phi_{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}},V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}}=-\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}},

the two factors of
q^{-\alpha}
being due to the inverse twists and the equality following from Lemma
2.10
and equation (
16
). Hence,
\Delta_{-}=-1
, from which it follows that
\Delta_{+}=1
.

Remark 2.17
.

Since relative modularity implies non-degenerate relative pre-modularity, Theorem
2.16
implies that
\mathcal{D}^{q}
defines invariants of decorated closed
3
-manifolds; see Section
1.4
. We expect that these invariants recover those of Bao and Ito
[
BI22
]
, who define invariants using a category closely related to
\mathcal{D}^{q}
, defined using Viro’s
q
-less subalgebra
of
U_{h}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))

[
Vir06
, §11.7]
.

2.7.
Relative modularity of
\mathcal{D}^{q}
for
q
a root of unity

Assume that
q
is a root of unity. In this section we endow
\mathcal{D}^{q}
with a relative modular structure different from that of Theorem
2.16
. Since the gradings involved depend on the parity of the order of the root of unity, we treat even and odd roots of unity separately. Many of the proofs are variations of those from Section
2.6
, so we will at points be brief.

2.7.1.
Odd roots of unity

Let
r\geq 3
be an odd positive integer and
\hbar=\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{r}
. Then
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is simple if and only if
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{r}{2}\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
. For each
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, let
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q}
be the full subcategory of modules whose weights are congruent to
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})
.

Proposition 2.18
.

The
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-graded category
\mathcal{D}^{q}\simeq\bigoplus_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}}\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
is generically semisimple with small symmetric subset
\mathsf{X}=\{(\bar{0},\bar{0}),(\bar{\frac{1}{2}},\bar{0})\}
. Moreover, if
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, then
\{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\mid(\alpha,a,\bar{p})\in\bar{\alpha}\times\bar{a}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\}
is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
.

Proof.

Let
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
and
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
non-zero. Since
\bar{\alpha}\neq\bar{0},\bar{\frac{1}{2}}
, a highest weight vector
v\in V
generates a submodule
\langle v\rangle\subset V
isomorphic to
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
for some
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
and lift
(\alpha,a)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
of
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})
. In particular,
\alpha\notin\frac{r}{2}\mathbb{Z}
and
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is simple and injective by Proposition
2.5
.
Thus, there is a splitting
V\simeq V^{\prime}\oplus\langle v\rangle
. Iterating this process proves the proposition.
∎

Let
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. For
(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}
, we compute

\theta_{\varepsilon(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{p}}}=q^{-2nn^{\prime}r^{2}+nr}\operatorname{Id}_{\varepsilon(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{p}}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\varepsilon(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{p}}}.

It follows easily from this that
\{\varepsilon(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{p}}\}_{(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}
defines a free realisation of
\mathsf{Z}
in
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{0},\bar{0})}
.
For each
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
, we compute

c_{V,\varepsilon(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{s}}}\circ c_{\varepsilon(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{s}},V}=q^{-2(n\bar{a}r+n^{\prime}\bar{\alpha}r)}\operatorname{Id}_{\varepsilon(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{s}}\otimes V}.

Thus,
\psi:\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
((\bar{\alpha},\bar{a}),(n,n^{\prime},\bar{s}))\mapsto q^{-2(n\bar{a}+n^{\prime}\bar{\alpha})r}
, is a bicharacter satisfying equation (
1
). Let
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
with chosen lift
(\alpha_{0},a_{0})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
. The set

\Theta(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})=\{V(\alpha_{0}+i,a_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}}\mid i,j\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}\}

satisfies
\Theta(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\otimes\sigma(\mathsf{Z})=\{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\mid(\alpha,a,\bar{p})\in\bar{\alpha}\times\bar{a}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\}
, which is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
by Proposition
2.18
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
and
i,j,k\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}
.

(1)

If
i\neq 0
, then
\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})},V(i,j)_{\bar{p}}}=0
.

(2)

The equality
\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})},P(0,j)_{\bar{p}}}=-\delta_{j,0}(q-q^{-1})r^{2}x_{0,0,\bar{0}}
holds.

(3)

If
i\neq 0
, then
\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})},V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}\otimes P(0,k)_{\bar{0}}}=0
.

Proof.

(1)

Using Lemma
2.10
and equation (
16
), we compute

\displaystyle\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})},V(i,j)_{\bar{p}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n=0}^{r-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta_{0}+m,b_{0}+n)_{\bar{0}})\Phi_{V(\beta_{0}+m,b_{0}+n)_{\bar{0}},V(i,j)_{\bar{p}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n=0}^{r-1}(q^{\beta_{0}+m}-q^{-\beta_{0}-m})^{-1}q^{-2(j(\beta_{0}+m)+i(b_{0}+n)}q^{\beta_{0}+m}(1-q^{2i})\operatorname{Id}_{V(i,j)_{\bar{p}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{r-1}\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{r-1}q^{-2in}\Big)(q^{\beta_{0}+m}-q^{-\beta_{0}-m})^{-1}q^{-2(j(\beta_{0}+m)+ib_{0}}q^{\beta_{0}+m}(1-q^{2i})\operatorname{Id}_{V(i,j)_{\bar{p}}}.

Since
i\neq 0
and
r
is odd,
r
does not divide
2i
, whence
q^{-2i}\neq 1
. The sum over
n
therefore vanishes.

(2)

Lemma
2.10
gives

\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})},P(0,j)_{\bar{0}}}=-(q-q^{-1})\sum_{n=0}^{r-1}\sum_{m=0}^{r-1}q^{-2j(\beta_{0}+m)}x_{(0,j,\bar{0})}.

If
j\neq 0
, then the sum over
m
vanishes for the same reason as in the previous part.

(3)

The isomorphisms (
14
) and (
15
) give

V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}\otimes P(0,k)_{\bar{0}}\simeq V(i,j+k-1)_{\bar{1}}\oplus V(i,j+k)^{\oplus 2}_{\bar{0}}\oplus V(i,j+k+1)_{\bar{1}}.

The desired equality therefore follows from the first part of the lemma and additivity of
\Phi
in its right index.∎

Theorem 2.20
.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is a modular
\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
-category relative to
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{X}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
with relative modularity parameter
\zeta=-r^{2}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is TQFT finite.

Proof.

Relative pre-modularity and TQFT finiteness are proved as in Theorem
2.16
, the only difference being that Proposition
2.18
is used in place of Proposition
2.13
. To find a relative modularity parameter
\zeta
, let
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a}),(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
. In the notation of equation (
2
), take

\Omega_{h}=\Omega_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})}=\sum_{i,j=0}^{r-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta_{0}+i,b_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}})\cdot V(\beta_{0}+i,b_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}}

and
V_{i}=V(\alpha_{0}+i_{1},a_{0}+j_{1})_{\bar{0}}
and
V_{j}=V(\alpha_{0}+i_{2},a_{0}+j_{2})_{\bar{0}}
for some
i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}
. Write
M
for
V(\alpha_{0}+i_{1},a_{0}+j_{1})_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha_{0}+i_{2},a_{0}+j_{2})^{*}_{\bar{0}}
, so that the morphism corresponding to the left hand side of equation (
2
) is
f\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(M)
. Using the isomorphisms (
14
) and (
15
), we have

I:M\xrightarrow{\sim}\begin{cases}V(i_{1}-i_{2},j_{1}-j_{2}+1)_{\bar{0}}\oplus V(i_{1}-i_{2},j_{1}-j_{2})_{\bar{1}}&\mbox{if }i_{1}\neq i_{2},\\
P(0,j_{1}-j_{2})_{\bar{0}}&\mbox{if }i_{1}=i_{2}.\end{cases}

If
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
, then

\displaystyle I^{*}f

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha_{0}+i_{1},a_{0}+j_{1})_{\bar{0}})\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})},V(i_{1}-i_{2},j_{1}-j_{2}+1)_{\bar{0}}\oplus V(i_{1}-i_{2},j_{1}-j_{2})_{\bar{1}})}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha_{0}+i_{1},a_{0}+j_{1})_{\bar{0}})\cdot

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=0}^{r-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta_{0}+i,b_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}})\left(\Phi_{V(\beta_{0}+i,b_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}},V(i_{1}-i_{2},j_{1}-j_{2}+1)_{\bar{0}}}+\Phi_{V(\beta_{0}+i,b_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}},V(i_{1}-i_{2},j_{1}-j_{2})_{\bar{1}})}\right),

which vanishes by part (
1
) of Lemma
2.19
.
If instead
i_{1}=i_{2}
, then

\displaystyle I^{*}f

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha_{0}+i_{1},a_{0}+j_{1})_{\bar{0}})\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})},P(0,j_{1}-j_{2})_{\bar{0}}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{if }j_{1}\neq j_{2},\\
-r^{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha_{0}+i_{1},a_{0}+j_{1})_{\bar{0}})(q-q^{-1})x_{(0,0,\bar{0})}&\mbox{if }j_{1}=j_{2},\end{cases}

the final equality following from part (
2
) of Lemma
2.19
. Using Lemma
2.12
, we conclude that
f=-r^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V(\alpha_{0}+i_{1},a_{0}+j_{1})_{\bar{0}}}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V(\alpha_{0}+i_{1},a_{0}+j_{1})_{\bar{0}}}
when
j_{1}=j_{2}
. It follows that
\zeta=-r^{2}
.
∎

Let
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
. The left hand side of the diagram in Definition
1.10
which defines
\Delta_{-}
is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=0}^{r-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha_{0}+i,a_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}})q^{2(\alpha_{0}+i)(a_{0}+j)-(\alpha_{0}+i)}q^{-\alpha_{0}}\Phi_{V(\alpha_{0}+i,a_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}},V(\alpha_{0},0)_{\bar{0}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=0}^{r-1}(q^{\alpha_{0}+i}-q^{-\alpha_{0}-i})^{-1}q^{2i(a_{0}+j)-(\alpha_{0}+i)}q^{i}(1-q^{2\alpha_{0}})\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha_{0},0)_{\bar{0}}}.

The sum over
j
is zero unless
i=0
, so that the last expression is equal to
-r\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha,0)_{\bar{0}}}
.
Hence,
\Delta_{-}=-r
, from which it follows that
\Delta_{+}=r
.

2.7.2.
Even roots of unity

Let
r\geq 2
be an even positive integer and
\hbar=\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{r}
. Then
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}
is simple if and only if
\alpha\notin\mathbb{C}\setminus r\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
. For each
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, denote by
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q}
the full subcategory of modules whose weights are congruent to
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})
.

Proposition 2.21
.

The
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-graded category
\mathcal{D}^{q}\simeq\bigoplus_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}}\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
is generically semisimple with small symmetric subset
\mathsf{X}=\{(\bar{0},\bar{0})\}
. Moreover, if
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, then
\{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\mid(\alpha,a,\bar{p})\in\bar{\alpha}\times\bar{a}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\}
is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
.

Proof.

This is proved in the same way as Proposition
2.18
.
∎

Let

\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. Then

\{\varepsilon(2nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{p}}\}_{(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}
is a free realisation of
\mathsf{Z}
in
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{0},\bar{0})}
and

c_{V,\varepsilon(2nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{s}}}\circ c_{\varepsilon(2nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{s}},V}=q^{-2(2n\bar{a}r+n^{\prime}\bar{\alpha}r)}\operatorname{Id}_{\varepsilon(2nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{s}}\otimes V}

for any
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
. Thus,
\psi:\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,

((\bar{\alpha},\bar{a}),(n,n^{\prime},\bar{s}))\mapsto q^{-2(2n\bar{a}+n^{\prime}\bar{\alpha})r}
, is a bicharacter satisfying equation (
1
). Let
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
with chosen lift
(\alpha_{0},a_{0})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
. The set

\Theta(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})=\{V(\alpha_{0}+i,a_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}}\mid i,j\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}\}

satisfies
\Theta(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})\otimes\sigma(\mathsf{Z})=\{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}}\mid(\alpha,a,\bar{p})\in\bar{\alpha}\times\bar{a}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\}
, which is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{D}^{q}_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
by Proposition
2.21
.

Lemma
2.19
holds in the present setting with only obvious modifications in its proof. For example, part (
1
) now follows from the fact that since
r
does not divide
i
and
q
is a primitive
2r
th
root of unity,
q^{-2i}\neq 1
.

Theorem 2.22
.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is a TQFT finite modular
\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
-category relative to
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{X}=\{(\bar{0},\bar{0})\}
with relative modularity parameter
\zeta=-r^{2}
.

Proof.

This is proved as in Theorem
2.20
, the only difference being that Proposition
2.21
is used in place of Proposition
2.18
.
∎

A calculation analogous to that of Section
2.7.1
gives
\Delta_{\pm}=\pm r
.

2.8.
Integral weight modules

Let
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q}
be the full subcategory of modules whose
G
-weights are integral. The results of Sections
2.3
and
2.4
generalize directly to
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
with essentially the same proofs. In particular, there exists an m-trace on the ideal of projectives
{\mathcal{P}}^{\textnormal{int}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
which is unique up to a global scalar, which we normalize as for
\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
has a ribbon structure. The categories
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
also admit various relative modular structures, summarized below, which are integral counterparts of those of Sections
2.6
and
2.7
.

2.8.1.
Relative modularity of
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
for arbitrary
q

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}/\penalty\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
. For each
\bar{\alpha}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, let
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}_{\bar{\alpha}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
be the full subcategory of modules whose
E
-weights are congruent to
\bar{\alpha}
. Then
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-graded and generically semisimple with
\mathsf{X}=\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}/\penalty\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
. The objects
\{\varepsilon(\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}n}{\hbar},n^{\prime})_{\bar{p}}\}_{(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}
define a free realisation of
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
in
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}_{\bar{0}}
and
\psi:\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
(\bar{\alpha},(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p}))\mapsto q^{-2\bar{\alpha}n^{\prime}}
, is a bicharacter satisfying equation (
1
). For each
\bar{\alpha}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, fix a lift
\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
and define
\Theta(\bar{\alpha})=\{V(\alpha_{0},0)_{\bar{0}}\}
. Then
\Theta(\bar{\alpha})\otimes\sigma(\mathsf{Z})
is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}_{\bar{\alpha}}
.

Theorem 2.23
.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
is a TQFT finite modular
\mathbb{C}/\penalty\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z},\mathsf{X}=\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}/\penalty\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z})
with relative modularity parameter
\zeta=-1
.

The stabilization coefficients are
\Delta_{\pm}=\pm 1
.

Remark 2.24
.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{q}
does not admit a relative pre-modular structure with respect to the smaller grading
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}/\penalty\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
, since no compatible bicharacter
\psi
exists.

2.8.2.
Relative modularity of
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
for
q
a root of unity

We treat the case of odd roots of unity, leaving the case of even roots of unity to the reader. Let
r\geq 3
be an odd positive integer,
\hbar=\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{r}
and
q=e^{\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{r}}
. Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
. For each
\bar{\alpha}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, let
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}_{\bar{\alpha}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
be the full subcategory of modules whose
E
-weights are congruent to
\bar{\alpha}
. Then
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-graded and generically semisimple with
\mathsf{X}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
. The objects
\{\varepsilon(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{p}}\}_{(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}
define a free realisation of
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
in
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}_{\bar{0}}
and
\psi:\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
(\bar{\alpha},(n,n^{\prime},\bar{s}))\mapsto q^{-2n^{\prime}\bar{\alpha}r}
, is a bicharacter satisfying equation (
1
). For each
\bar{\alpha}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, fix a lift
\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
and define
\Theta(\bar{\alpha})=\{V(\alpha_{0}+i,j)_{\bar{0}}\mid i,j\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}\}
. Then
\Theta(\bar{\alpha})\otimes\sigma(\mathsf{Z})
is a completely reduced dominating set for
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}_{\bar{\alpha}}
.

Theorem 2.25
.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
is a modular
\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
-category relative to
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{X}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
with relative modularity parameter
\zeta=-r^{2}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
is TQFT finite.

The stabilization coefficients are
\Delta_{\pm}=\pm r
.

3.
A TQFT for arbitrary
q

Work in the setting of Section
2.6
, so that
q=e^{\hbar}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\pm 1\}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is given the relative modular structure of Theorem
2.16
.
Fix
{\mathscr{D}}=\sqrt{-1}
so that
\delta=-\sqrt{-1}
. The braiding of
\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is determined by the pairing

\gamma:\mathsf{Z}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\},\qquad((b_{1},\bar{p}_{1}),(b_{2},\bar{p}_{2}))\mapsto(-1)^{\bar{p}_{1}\bar{p}_{2}}.

In this section we study the TQFT
\mathcal{Z}:\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}\rightarrow\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
associated to
\mathcal{D}^{q}
by Theorem
1.12
. Set
g_{0}=1
and
V_{g_{0}}=V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}
, in the notation of Section
1.5
.

Let
(M,T,\omega,n):\emptyset\rightarrow\emptyset
be a morphism in
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
. If
b_{1}(M)\geq 1
, then
\mathcal{Z}(M,T,\omega,n)
is holomorphic in
\omega
. Indeed, in view of the definition of
F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(R)
from Section
1.4
, it suffices to note that both
\langle T_{V}\rangle
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{p}})
are holomorphic in
\omega
; the latter follows from equation (
16
).

3.1.
The state space of a generic surface of genus at least one

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\omega)
be a decorated connected surface without marked points. Assume that the genus of
\Sigma
is
g\geq 1
.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
is not in the image of the canonical map
H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathsf{X})\rightarrow H^{1}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
, then there exists a handlebody
\eta
bounding
\Sigma
which contains an oriented trivalent spine
\Gamma
with the property that
\omega(m_{e})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
for all edges
e
of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Since the small symmetric subset
\mathsf{X}
is in fact a subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, the lemma can be proved in the same way as
[
BCGPM16
, Proposition 6.5]
which, moreover, proves that the spine
\Gamma
may be taken to be that of Section
1.6
.
∎

Continue to denote by
\Gamma
the spine from Section
1.6
. Consider the graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}
obtained by modifying
\Gamma
near the vertex incident to
e_{1}
,
e_{g}
and
f_{2g-3}
:

\widetilde{\Gamma}=\qquad\hbox to192.43pt{\vbox to97.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-92.50165pt\lower-63.36017pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Let
H=\{(0,0)_{\bar{0}},(0,-1)_{\bar{1}}\}=\{0,\delta\}
, interpreted below as labeling the two summands on the right hand side of the decomposition (
14
).

Definition 3.2
.

A
coloring of degree
k\in\mathsf{Z}
of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is a function
\mathsf{Col}:\textnormal{Edge}(\widetilde{\Gamma})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{q}
assigning to each
e\in\textnormal{Edge}(\Gamma)
a simple module
V(\alpha_{e},a_{e})_{\bar{p}_{e}}
, where
(\alpha_{e},a_{e},\bar{p}_{e})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
is of degree
\omega(m_{e})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, that is,
\alpha_{e}=\omega(m_{e})
, and

\mathsf{Col}(e_{-1}^{1})=V_{g_{0}}=\mathsf{Col}(e_{-1}^{-1}),\qquad\mathsf{Col}(e_{-1}^{0})=\sigma(k)

such that the following
Balancing Condition
holds: at each trivalent vertex of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, the algebraic sum of the colors of the incident edges is in
H
.

Fix a lift
\tilde{\omega}\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z})
of
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
and let

\mathfrak{C}_{k}=\{\mbox{degree $k$ colorings of $\widetilde{\Gamma}$}\mid\mathsf{Col}(e_{i})=\tilde{\omega}(m_{e_{i}}),\;i=1,\dots,g\}.

Set
\mathfrak{C}=\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathsf{Z}}\mathfrak{C}_{k}
. A coloring
c\in\mathfrak{C}_{k}
defines a vector
v_{c}=(\tilde{\eta},\widetilde{\Gamma}_{c},\omega,0)\in\mathcal{Z}_{-k}({\mathcal{S}})
, as explained in Section
1.6
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\omega)
be a decorated connected surface without marked points and underlying surface of genus
g\geq 1
such that
\omega
is not in the image of the canonical map
H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathsf{X})\rightarrow H^{1}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
. Assume that
\omega
is such that
\omega(e_{1})+g_{0}\not\in\mathsf{X}
. Then
\{v_{c}\mid c\in\mathfrak{C}_{k}\}
spans
\mathcal{Z}_{-k}({\mathcal{S}})
. Moreover,
\mathcal{Z}_{-k}({\mathcal{S}})
is trivial unless
k=(d,\bar{d})
for some
d\in[-g,g]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
, there exists a handlebody
\eta
containing a generically colored spine
\Gamma
. Proposition
1.17
shows that
\mathcal{Z}_{-k}({\mathcal{S}})
is spanned by vectors
\{(\tilde{\eta},\Gamma^{\prime}_{c},\omega,0)\}_{c}
, where
c
is a finite projective
\mathcal{D}^{q}
-coloring of the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
from Section
1.6
. Since
\Gamma
is generically colored, all projective indecomposables in any such coloring are in fact simple. Moreover, the coupon in
\Gamma^{\prime}
is colored by a morphism
l\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V_{f_{0}},V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(k)\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*})
, where
V_{f_{0}}
is the simple module coloring
f_{0}
. The associated vector is in the span of vectors of the form
\{v_{c}\mid c\in\mathfrak{C}_{k}\}
, as follows from the skein equivalences

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-50.0,-40.0){\mbox{\tiny$e_{1}$}}\put(-62.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$e_{g+1}$}}\put(-15.0,10.0){\mbox{\tiny$f_{0}$}}\put(-40.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$g_{0}$}}\put(-30.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$\sigma(k)$}}\put(-10.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$g_{0}$}}\put(-38.0,-15.0){\mbox{\tiny$h$}}\put(-23.0,33.0){\mbox{\tiny$l$}}\qquad=\qquad\sum_{h_{1}}\;\;\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-42.0,-40.0){\mbox{\tiny$e_{1}$}}\put(-60.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$e_{g+1}$}}\put(-40.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$g_{0}$}}\put(-29.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$\sigma(k)$}}\put(-10.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$g_{0}$}}\put(-32.0,-15.0){\mbox{\tiny$h_{1}$}}\qquad=\qquad\sum_{W_{i}}\sum_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}}\qquad\;\;\;\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-30.0,-43.0){\mbox{\tiny$e_{1}$}}\put(-60.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$e_{g+1}$}}\put(-40.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$g_{0}$}}\put(-30.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$\sigma(k)$}}\put(-10.0,65.0){\mbox{\tiny$g_{0}$}}\put(-88.0,20.0){\mbox{\tiny$W_{i}\otimes\sigma(k)^{-1}$}}\put(-45.0,-10.0){\mbox{\tiny$W_{i}$}}\put(-20.0,-22.0){\mbox{\tiny$h_{1}$}}\put(-32.0,6.0){\mbox{\tiny$h_{2}$}}\put(-42.0,35.0){\mbox{\tiny$h_{3}$}}.

Here we use that that the linear map

\bigoplus_{W_{i}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V_{f_{0}},W_{i}\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(W_{i},(W_{i}\otimes\sigma(k)^{-1})\otimes\sigma(k))\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(W_{i}\otimes\sigma(k)^{-1},V_{f_{2g-2}}\otimes V_{g_{0}})\\
\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V_{f_{0}},V_{f_{2g-2}}\otimes V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(k)\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*})

given by
f\otimes g\otimes h\mapsto(h\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{V_{g_{0}}^{*}\otimes\sigma(k)})\circ(g\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{V_{g_{0}}^{*}})\circ f
is surjective, where the sum runs over all simple modules which satisfy the Balancing Condition at each trivalent vertex (coupon). Explicitly,
W_{i}
and
W_{i}\otimes\sigma(k)^{-1}
are the colors of
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{g+1}^{\prime}
, respectively.

Next, we study the set
\mathfrak{C}
. Assume for the moment that
g\geq 2
. An element of
\mathfrak{C}
can be constructed as follows. First, for each
i=1,\dots,g
, color
e_{i}
by
(\alpha_{i},a_{i})_{\bar{p}_{i}}=\tilde{\omega}(m_{e_{i}})
. Next, color the edges
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{2g-3}
recursively, beginning with
f_{1}
, so as to satisfy the Balancing Conditions. In this way, the colors
\{(\beta_{i},b_{i})_{\bar{q}_{i}}\}_{i}
of
\{f_{i}\}_{i}
are determined by a tuple
(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{2g-3})\in\{0,1\}^{\times 2g-3}
through the initial conditions

\begin{cases}\beta_{1}=\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\\
b_{1}=a_{1}-a_{2}+\epsilon_{1}\end{cases}

and the recursive system

\begin{cases}\beta_{2i}=\beta_{2i-1}+\alpha_{i+1}\\
b_{2i}=b_{2i-1}+a_{i+1}-\epsilon_{2i}\end{cases},\qquad 1\leq i\leq g-2

and

\begin{cases}\beta_{2i+1}+\alpha_{i+2}=\beta_{2i}\\
b_{2i+1}+a_{i+2}-\epsilon_{2i+1}=b_{2i}\\
\end{cases},\qquad 1\leq i\leq g-2.

We solve these equations to obtain

\begin{cases}\beta_{2i}=\alpha_{1}\\
b_{2i}=a_{1}+\sum_{j=1}^{2i}(-1)^{j}\epsilon_{j}\end{cases},\qquad 1\leq i\leq g-2

and

\begin{cases}\beta_{2i+1}=\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{i+2}\\
b_{2i+1}=a_{1}-a_{i+2}+\sum_{j=1}^{2i+1}(-1)^{j}\epsilon_{j}\end{cases},\qquad 0\leq i\leq g-2.

Similar considerations give for the parities

\bar{q}_{2i}=\bar{p}_{1}+\sum_{j=1}^{2i}\bar{\epsilon}_{j},\qquad 1\leq i\leq g-2

and

\bar{q}_{2i+1}=\bar{p}_{1}+\bar{p}_{i+2}+\sum_{j=1}^{2i+1}\bar{\epsilon}_{j},\qquad 0\leq i\leq g-2.

It remains to color the edges
e_{g+1}
,
e_{g+1}^{\prime}
and
e_{1}^{\prime}
. Writing
k=(c,\bar{r})
, these colors are subject only to the four Balancing Conditions:

(\alpha_{1},a_{1})_{\bar{p}_{1}}+g_{0}=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime})_{\bar{p}_{1}^{\prime}}+\epsilon\delta,

(\alpha_{g+1}^{\prime},a_{g+1}^{\prime})_{\bar{p}_{g+1}^{\prime}}+(0,c)_{\bar{r}}=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime})_{\bar{p}_{1}^{\prime}}

(\alpha_{g+1}^{\prime},a_{g+1}^{\prime})_{\bar{p}_{g+1}^{\prime}}+\epsilon^{\prime}\delta=(\alpha_{g+1},a_{g+1})_{\bar{p}_{g+1}}+g_{0}

(\alpha_{g+1},a_{g+1})_{\bar{p}_{g+1}^{\prime}}=(\alpha_{g},a_{g})_{\bar{p}_{g}^{\prime}}+(\beta_{2g-3},b_{2g-3})_{\bar{q}_{2g-3}^{\prime}}.

Here
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\mu\in\{0,1\}
. These equations hold if and only if

c=\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon-\mu-\sum_{j=1}^{2g-3}\epsilon_{j},\qquad\bar{r}=\bar{\epsilon}^{\prime}+\bar{\epsilon}+\overline{\mu}+\sum_{j=1}^{2g-3}\bar{\epsilon}_{j}.

and

\alpha_{g+1}=\alpha_{1},\qquad a_{g+1}=a_{1}+\mu+\sum_{j=1}^{2g-3}(-1)^{j}\epsilon_{j},\qquad\bar{p}_{g+1}=\bar{p}_{1}+\overline{\mu}+\sum_{j=1}^{2g-3}\bar{\epsilon}_{j}.

Setting
d=\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon-\mu+\sum_{j=1}^{2g-2}(-1)^{j}\epsilon_{j}
, we have constructed a degree
k=(d,\bar{d})
coloring of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
. It is immediate that the above construction recovers each element of
\mathfrak{C}
. In particular,
\mathfrak{C}_{k}
is empty unless
k
is of the form
(d,\bar{d})
for some
d\in[-g,g]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.

The case
g=1
, where
e_{1}=f_{0}
, can be treated in the same way, leading to the same conclusions. We omit the details.
∎

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{C}_{k}
the subset of colorings of degree
k
with
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}=0
, in the notation of the proof of Theorem
3.3
. Set
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}=\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathsf{Z}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{k}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Work in the setting of Theorem
3.3
. For each
d\in[-(g-1),g-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}
, the vectors
\{v_{c}\mid c\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{(d,\bar{d})}\}
form a linearly independent subset of
\mathcal{Z}_{-(d,\bar{d})}({\mathcal{S}})
of cardinality
{2g-2\choose g-1-|d|}
.

Proof.

Let
k=(d,\bar{d})
. We prove linear independence by modifying the argument from
[
BCGPM16
, Proposition 6.7]
. Let
\bar{v}_{c}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(\Sigma\sqcup\widehat{S}_{-k})
be the vector obtained from
v_{c}
by reversing the orientation of
\eta\setminus\mathring{B}
and dualizing the colors of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. The pairing
\langle\overline{v}_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}}\rangle
can be computed as a connected sum of
\Theta
-graphs, as in
[
BCGPM16
, Figure 5]
. If
c_{1}\neq c_{2}
, then the Balancing Conditions are not met for some of these
\Theta
-graphs and
\langle\overline{v}_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}}\rangle=0
. If instead
c_{1}=c_{2}
, then all Balancing Conditions are met and a direct calculation shows that the value of each
\Theta
-graph is non-zero, whence
\langle\overline{v}_{c},v_{c}\rangle\neq 0
. This proves linear independence.

Turning to the cardinality statement, note that if
d\geq 0
, then for each
d\leq l\leq g-1
there are
{g-1\choose k+d}{g-1\choose l}
elements of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{(d,\bar{d})}
for which exactly
l+d
of the elements
\{\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{4},\dots,\epsilon_{2g-4},\mu\}
are equal to
1
; in such cases necessarily
l
of the elements
\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{3},\dots,\epsilon_{2g-3}\}
are equal to
1
. Together with similar considerations for
d\leq 0
, this gives

|\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{(d,\bar{d})}|=\sum_{l=0}^{g-1-|d|}{g-1\choose l+|d|}{g-1\choose l}={2g-2\choose g-1-|d|}.\qed

3.2.
Verlinde formula

We compute the value of
\mathcal{Z}
on trivial circle bundles over surfaces and relate the result to dimensions and Euler characteristics of state spaces of surfaces. This allows us to deduce a Verlinde formulate for
\mathcal{Z}
. Our approach is motivated by that of
[
BCGPM16
, §6.3]
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\},\omega,\mathcal{L})
be a decorated connected surface of genus
g\geq 0
. Since the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
does not play a role in this section, we henceforth ignore it. For each
\beta\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
, let

{\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta}=(\Sigma\times S^{1},T=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times S^{1},\omega\oplus\beta),

where we use the canonical isomorphism
H^{1}((\Sigma\times S^{1})\setminus T;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})\simeq H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus\{p_{i}\};\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
to extend
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus\{p_{i}\};\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
to
\omega\oplus\beta
. Assume all colors of
{\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta}
have degree in
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})
can be computed by the following surgery presentation:

L=\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-205.0,55.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\beta_{1}}$}}\put(-265.0,40.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\alpha_{1}}$}}\put(-74.0,55.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\beta_{g}}$}}\put(-135.0,40.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\alpha_{g}}$}}\put(-5.0,-29.0){{\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\beta}$}}}\put(-180.0,-25.0){\mbox{\tiny$X_{1}$}}\put(-120.0,-25.0){\mbox{\tiny$X_{n}$}}\;\;.

Here
X_{i}=V(\mu_{i},m_{i})_{\bar{q}_{i}}
is the color of
p_{i}
and
\alpha_{i}=\omega([a_{i}]),\beta_{i}=\omega([b_{i}])\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
for a symplectic basis
\{[a_{i}],[b_{i}]\}_{i=1}^{g}
of
H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
. By applying equation (
2
) first to the
\Omega_{\alpha_{i}}
-colored strand and then to the
\Omega_{\beta_{i}}
-colored strand, we can simplify the surgery presentation using the equalities

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-13.0,35.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\alpha_{i}}$}}\put(-15.0,-10.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\beta_{i}}$}}\put(-42.0,7.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\beta}$}}=-\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta,0)^{*}_{\bar{0}})^{-1}\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-1.0,-12.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\beta_{i}}$}}\put(-22.0,-31.0){\mbox{\tiny$\Omega_{\beta}$}}\put(-55.0,-23.0){\mbox{\tiny$V(\beta,0)_{\bar{0}}$}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta,0)^{*}_{\bar{0}})^{-2}\operatorname{Id}_{\Omega_{\beta}}.

Applying this simplification for
i=1,\dots,g
reduces the link
L
to an
\Omega_{\beta}
-colored unknot encircled by
n
pairwise unlinked unknots colored by
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
. After noting that
\sigma(L)=0
and using equation (
17
), evaluating the simplified diagram gives

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})={\mathscr{D}}^{-2g-2}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta,0)_{\bar{0}})^{2-2g}S^{\prime}(\{X_{i}\},\Omega_{\beta}),

where
S^{\prime}(\{X_{i}\},\Omega_{\beta})
is the scalar associated to the
n
unlinked unknots. Set

\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i},\qquad m=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i},\qquad\bar{q}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{q}_{i}.

We use Lemma
2.10
to compute

S^{\prime}(\{X_{i}\},\Omega_{\beta})=(-1)^{n+\bar{q}}q^{\beta(n-2m)+\mu}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta,0)_{\bar{0}})^{-n}.

Upon using equation (
16
), this gives the final result

(19)

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})=(-1)^{g-1+n+\bar{q}}q^{\beta(n-2m)+\mu}(q^{\beta}-q^{-\beta})^{2g-2+n}.

Since
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})
is holomorphic in
\omega
, we conclude that equation (
19
) in fact holds whenever the decorated surface
{\mathcal{S}}
is admissible.

Define the generating function of
\mathsf{Z}
-graded dimensions of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
by

\dim_{(t,s)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\sum_{(b,\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}(-1)^{\bar{p}}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}_{(b,\bar{p})}({\mathcal{S}})t^{b}s^{\bar{p}}.

The sign
(-1)^{\bar{p}}
reflects the braiding of
\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

The next result is a Verlinde formula for
\mathcal{Z}
.

Theorem 3.5
.

The equality
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})=\dim_{(q^{-2\beta},1)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
holds.

Proof.

Since this is proved similarly to
[
BCGPM16
, Theorem 5.9]
, we will be brief. Let
\{e_{i}\}
be a homogeneous basis of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. Write
(b_{i},\bar{p}_{i})\in\mathsf{Z}
for the degree of
e_{i}
and
\{e^{i}\}
for the corresponding basis of
\mathcal{Z}(\overline{{\mathcal{S}}})
. The equality

{\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta}=\cap_{{\mathcal{S}}}\circ\tau_{\sqcup}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\beta}\sqcup\operatorname{Id}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}})\circ\cup_{{\mathcal{S}}}

holds in
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
, where
\cap_{{\mathcal{S}}}:{\mathcal{S}}\sqcup\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}\rightarrow\emptyset
and
\cup_{{\mathcal{S}}}:\emptyset\rightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}\sqcup{\mathcal{S}}
denote the cylinder on
{\mathcal{S}}
, suitably interpreted,
\tau_{\sqcup}
is the braiding of
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
and
\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\beta}
is as in Section
1.5
. Denoting by
u_{0}
a basis of
\mathcal{Z}(\emptyset)\simeq\mathbb{C}
, we compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})u_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\cap_{{\mathcal{S}}})\circ\mathcal{Z}(\tau_{\sqcup})\circ(\mathcal{Z}(\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\beta})\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{Z}(\overline{{\mathcal{S}}})})\circ\mathcal{Z}(\cup_{{\mathcal{S}}})(u_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}q^{-2\beta b_{i}}\mathcal{Z}(\cap_{{\mathcal{S}}})\circ P^{\sqcup}\circ\tau(e_{i}\otimes e^{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}(-1)^{\bar{p}_{i}}q^{-2\beta b_{i}}\mathcal{Z}(\cap_{{\mathcal{S}}})\circ P^{\sqcup}(e^{i}\otimes e_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{(b,\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}(-1)^{\bar{p}}q^{-2\beta b}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}_{(b,\bar{p})}({\mathcal{S}})\big)u_{0},

where
P^{\sqcup}
is the monoidal coherence data for
\mathcal{Z}
. The second equality follows from the discussion in Section
1.5
surrounding the construction of the refined grading of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
.
∎

The right hand side of the equality in Theorem
3.5
can be written more explicitly as

\dim_{(q^{-2\beta},1)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\sum_{b\in\mathbb{C}}\chi(\mathcal{Z}_{(b,\bullet)}({\mathcal{S}}))q^{-2\beta b},

where
\chi
denotes the Euler characteristic of a
\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector space.

Example 3.6
.

When
n=0
equation (
19
) becomes
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})=(-1)^{g-1}(q^{\beta}-q^{-\beta})^{2g-2}
. If
g\geq 1
, then Theorem
3.3
implies

\dim_{(t,s)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\sum_{b=-g}^{g}(-1)^{\bar{b}}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}_{(b,\bar{b})}({\mathcal{S}})t^{b}s^{\bar{b}}.

Applying Theorem
3.5
, we conclude that

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\dim_{(-1,1)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\lim_{\beta\rightarrow\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{2\hbar}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})=2^{2g-2}

and

\chi(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}))=\dim_{(1,1)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})\\
=\lim_{\beta\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{\beta})\\
=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }g=1,\\
0&\mbox{if }g\geq 2.\end{cases}\qed

We can now give a complete description of state spaces of generic surfaces of genus at least one.

Corollary 3.7
.

Work in the setting of Theorem
3.3
. The vectors
\{v_{c}\mid c\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}\}
are a basis of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. In particular,
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is supported in degrees
(d,\bar{d})
with
d\in[-(g-1),g-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Using the cardinality statement in Proposition
3.4
, we compute

|\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}|=\sum_{d=-(g-1)}^{g-1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{(d,\bar{d})}|=\sum_{d=-(g-1)}^{g-1}{2g-2\choose g-1-|d|}=2^{2g-2},

which is equal to
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
by Example
3.6
. The corollary now follows from the linear independence statement in Proposition
3.4
.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

As mentioned in Section
1.5
, up to natural isomorphism,
\mathcal{Z}
is independent of the particular choice of
g_{0}
and
V_{g_{0}}
used to define the decorated
2
-spheres
\widehat{S}_{k}
,
k\in\mathsf{Z}
. In particular, by choosing a suitable
g_{0}
, we see that the dimension statement in Corollary
3.7
holds for all generic
\omega
.

3.3.
The state space of the torus with non-generic cohomology class

Let
S^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}
be the unit circle and
\Sigma=S^{1}\times S^{1}
. Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\omega,\mathcal{L})
be a decoration of
\Sigma
without marked points. If
\omega
is generic, then Corollary
3.7
applies, showing that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is supported in
\mathsf{Z}
-degree zero and one dimensional. The goal of this section is to give s similarly complete description of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
in the non-generic case.

Proposition 3.9
.

Assume that there exists an oriented simple closed curve
\gamma
in
\Sigma
such that
\omega(\gamma)\in\mathsf{X}\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
. Then
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{S}})=2
.

Proof.

The Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
does not affect the proof, so we ignore it. Let
B^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}
be the closed unit disk,
\eta=B^{2}\times S^{1}
and
\overline{\eta}
the orientation reversal of
\eta
. Consider
\Gamma=\{0\}\times S^{1}
as a core of
\eta
.

Applying a diffeomorphism if necessary, we can assume that
\omega(m_{\Gamma})=\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\in\mathsf{X}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\Gamma_{V,f}
be the graph
\Gamma
colored by
V
with coupon
f\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(V)
. Noting that
\Gamma
coincides with the ribbon graph
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
of Proposition
1.18
, Proposition
2.6
implies that the vectors

(20)

\{\mathcal{P}_{n}=(\eta,\Gamma_{P(0)_{\bar{0}},\operatorname{Id}_{P(0)_{\bar{0}}}}),\mathcal{P}_{n,x}=(\eta,\Gamma_{P(0)_{\bar{0}},x_{0,\bar{0}}})\},

span
\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{S})
where, for ease of notation, we have omitted the constant
E
-weight
\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}
. To verify the linear independence of (
20
), we pair these vectors with various elements of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}({\mathcal{S}})
.

Let
[\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]
be the empty negatively oriented copy of
\eta
and
[S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]
the vector obtained from
[\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]
by changing the identification of
\partial\eta
with
S^{1}\times S^{1}
by the map
S:\Sigma\rightarrow\Sigma
given by
S(\theta,\theta^{\prime})=(-\theta^{\prime},\theta)
. Then
[S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{n}
is
S^{3}
with the core of
\eta
as a
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{0}}
-colored unknot. Using Lemma
2.11
, we find

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})={\mathscr{D}}^{-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{0}}}(\operatorname{Id}_{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{0}}})=0.

Replacing
\mathcal{P}_{n}
with
\mathcal{P}_{n,x}
gives

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{n,x})={\mathscr{D}}^{-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{0}}}(x_{\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0,\bar{0}})=-\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})^{-1}.

This shows that
\mathcal{P}_{n,x}
is non-zero with dual vector (with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle
)
\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})[S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]
.

Next, consider the pairing with
[\overline{\eta}_{-n}]
, whose core is colored by
\varepsilon(-\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{0}}
. The composition
[\overline{\eta}_{-n}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{n}
gives
S^{2}\times S^{1}
with a knot of the form
{p}\times S^{1}
colored by

P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{0}}\otimes\varepsilon(-\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{0}}\simeq P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}\simeq V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}^{*}.

The decorated manifold underlying
[\overline{\eta}_{-n}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{n}
is therefore skein equivalent to two parallel
V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}
-colored knots in
S^{2}\times S^{1}
with opposite orientation. This has a surgery presentation in
S^{3}
given by two parallel knots with opposite orientation colored by
V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}
encircled by an
\Omega_{1}
-colored unknot. Using the modularity condition (equation (
2
)) and Lemmas
2.11
and
2.12
, we compute

\displaystyle{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-n}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})

\displaystyle=
