\displaystyle{\mathscr{D}}^{-2}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}^{*}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}})(-1)\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(1,0)_{\bar{0}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\mathscr{D}}^{-2}(q-q^{-1})\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(1,0)_{\bar{0}})\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}(x_{0,0,\bar{0}})\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(1,0)_{\bar{0}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

Similarly, pairing
[\overline{\eta}_{-n}]
with
\mathcal{P}_{n,x}
gives a surgery presentation in
S^{3}
with a
P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}
-colored unknot with coupon
x_{0,0,\bar{0}}
encircled by an
\Omega_{1}
-colored unknot. This gives

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-n}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{n,x})=-{\mathscr{D}}^{-2}(q-q^{-1})\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(1,0)_{\bar{0}})\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}(x^{2}_{0,0,\bar{0}})\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(1,0)_{\bar{0}})^{-1}=0,

since
x^{2}_{0,0,\bar{0}}=0
. We conclude that (
20
) is a basis of
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
.

To complete the proof, recall the isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{S}})\simeq\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
of Proposition
1.18
.
∎

For later use, note that the proof of Proposition
3.9
shows that a basis dual to (
20
) is

(21)

\{[\overline{\eta}_{-n}],\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})[S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\}.

Next, we prove the vanishing of the summands
\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\mathcal{S}})
for non-zero
k
. To do so, we require the following preliminary result.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
with
G
-weight decomposition
V=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathbb{C}}V[a]
. Then
V
admits a
G
-weight basis
\mathcal{B}
with the property that if
v\in Y\cdot V[a]\cap\mathcal{B}
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
, then
v=Yw
for some
w\in V[a]\cap\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{D}^{q}
is Krull–Schmidt, it suffices to prove that statement for
V
indecomposable. In this case, the
G
-weight decomposition takes the form
V=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{N}V[a-j]
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with each
V[a-j]
non-zero. Choose a basis
\mathcal{B}^{a}=\{v_{1}^{a},\dots,v_{d_{a}}^{a}\}
of
V[a]
. Then
Y\cdot\mathcal{B}^{a}=\{Yv_{1}^{a},\dots,Yv_{d_{a}}^{a}\}
spans the subspace
Y\cdot V[a]\subset V[a-1]
. Let
\mathcal{B}^{Y,a}\subset Y\cdot\mathcal{B}^{a}
be a basis of
Y\cdot V[a]
and extend it to a basis
\mathcal{B}^{a-1}
of
V[a-1]
. In the same way, we construct from
\mathcal{B}^{a-1}
a basis of
\mathcal{B}^{a-2}
of
V[a-2]
which extends a basis
\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-1}\subset Y\cdot\mathcal{B}^{a-1}
of
Y\cdot V[a-1]
. Repeating, we obtain a basis
\mathcal{B}=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{N}\mathcal{B}^{a-j}
of
V
with the desired properties.
∎

Proposition 3.11
.

In the setting of Proposition
3.9
, we have
\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\mathcal{S}})=0
if
k\neq 0
.

Proof.

By Proposition
1.17
, the state space
\mathcal{Z}_{-k}({\mathcal{S}})
is spanned by finite projective
\mathcal{D}^{q}
-colorings of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Then
e_{1}
is colored by
P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}
and the coupon by a composition

P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}^{\otimes 2}\simeq P(0,1)_{\bar{1}}\oplus P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}^{\oplus 2}\oplus P(0,-1)_{\bar{1}}\rightarrow P\rightarrow V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(k)\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*}\simeq P(0,b)_{\bar{p}},

where
P
is a projective indecomposable of degree
0
and
k=(b,\bar{p})
. By Proposition
2.6
, this morphism is zero unless
(b,\bar{p})\in\{(0,\bar{0}),(\pm 1,\bar{1}),(\pm 2,\bar{0})\}
. The case
k=(0,\bar{0})
is dealt with in Proposition
3.9
. When
k=(\pm 2,\bar{0})
, the state space is spanned by vectors obtained by coloring
\Gamma^{\prime}
so that its coupon is colored by an element of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}^{\otimes 2},V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(\pm 2,\bar{0})\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*})
. When such a vector is paired with any vector, the obtained closed
3
-manifold has a surgery presentation in which the edges leaving the coupon are encircled by a surgery component. Thus, this surgery presentation is skien equivalent to a graph containing a coupon filled with a morphism in
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}^{\otimes 2},\sigma(\pm 2,\bar{0}))
, which is the zero space. Thus,
\mathcal{Z}_{(\pm 2,\bar{0})}({\mathcal{S}})
is zero.

Suppose then that
k=(1,\bar{1})
; the case
k=(-1,\bar{1})
is analogous. The state space
\mathcal{Z}_{(1,\bar{1})}({\mathcal{S}})
is spanned by the vector
v
obtained by coloring
\Gamma^{\prime}
with
P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}
. We claim that
v\in\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is the zero vector. By part (
2
) of Lemma
1.13
, it suffices to show that
\langle w,v\rangle=0
for all
w\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}({\mathcal{S}})
. By isotopy and skein equivalence, the pairing
\langle w,v\rangle
can be computed as a sum of evaluations of diagrams of the form

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-33.0,-17.0){\mbox{\tiny$\psi$}}\put(-33.0,22.0){\mbox{\tiny$\phi$}}\put(-65.0,20.0){\mbox{\tiny$V_{1}$}}\put(-47.0,2.0){\mbox{\tiny$V_{l}$}}\put(-75.0,-42.0){\mbox{\tiny$P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}$}}\put(-65.0,-11.0){\tiny\vdots}\put(5.0,0.0){\mbox{\tiny$\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{1}}$}}

for indecomposables
V_{1},\dots,V_{l}\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
of
E
-weight
0
and morphisms
\phi
,
\psi
. Note here the coupon labeled with
\phi
receives contributions from both the vector
w\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}({\mathcal{S}})
and possible surgery components coming from the pairing. Also, as in the previous step, the edge colored with
\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{1}}
is obtained from a skein relation removing a coupon with edges colored with
V_{g_{0}},\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{1}}
and
V_{g_{0}}^{*}
which in the pairing is encircled by a surgery component and thus can be simplified.

To prove that
\langle w,v\rangle=0
, it suffices to prove that, for any indecomposable
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
of
E
-weight
0
and
\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{1}},V^{*}\otimes V)
, the sub-diagram

(22)

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-35.0,-17.0){\mbox{\tiny$\phi$}}\put(-33.0,-5.0){\mbox{\tiny$V$}}\put(5.0,-35.0){\mbox{\tiny$P$}}\put(-65.0,-35.0){\mbox{\tiny$\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{1}}$}}

evaluates to zero. While we require this statement only for
P=P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}
, we prove that it holds for all
P
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
be indecomposable with
E
-weight
0
and
G
-weight space decomposition
V=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{N}V[a-i]
. The weight space
V[a]
is homogeneous, say of degree
\bar{s}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. Then
V[a-i]
is homogeneous of degree
\bar{s}+\bar{i}
. Fix a basis
\mathcal{B}=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{N}\mathcal{B}^{a-j}=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{N}\bigsqcup_{i}\{v_{i}^{a-j}\}
of
V
of the form guaranteed by Lemma
3.10
and its proof. Here and below we have written the
G
-weight of a vector as a superscript.

The image of a basis vector of
\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{1}}
under
\phi
takes the form

(23)

\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}}f_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes v_{i}^{a-j}\in V^{*}\otimes V

for some
f_{i}^{-a+j+1}\in V^{*}
. The restrictions on weights comes from the fact that
\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{1}}
is concentrated in
G
-degree
1
. Similar considerations for parity give
|f_{i}^{-a+j+1}|=\bar{s}+\bar{j}+\bar{1}
. With this notation, the portion of diagram (
22
)
below the braidings maps a homogeneous vector
p\in P
to

\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}}f_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes v_{i}^{a-j}\Big)\otimes p.

In view of the eventual application of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}
, weight considerations imply that the only terms of the double (inverse) braiding of
V
with
P
which can contribute to diagram (
22
) arise from the identity in the lower braiding and the term
-(q-q^{-1})(X\otimes Y)(K\otimes K^{-1})
in the upper braiding. It follows that
diagram (
22
) maps
p\in P
to

(24)

-(q-q^{-1})\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}(-1)^{\bar{s}+\bar{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}}f_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes Yv_{i}^{a-j}\Big)\right)\otimes Xp

which, since
Y^{2}=0
, is equal to

-(q-q^{-1})\sum_{j=0}^{N}(-1)^{\bar{s}+\bar{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-j+1}}f_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes Yv_{i}^{a-j}\Big)\otimes Xp.

Fix
j\in\{0,\dots,N\}
. A direct computation shows

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-j+1}}f_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes Yv_{i}^{a-j}\Big)=(-1)^{\bar{s}+\bar{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V}\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-j+1}}Yf_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes v_{i}^{a-j}\Big).

We claim that the vector
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-j+1}}Yf_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes v_{i}^{a-j}
is in fact zero. This will imply that the previous evaluations, and hence (
24
), vanish, thereby completing the proof. To prove the claim, note that since
Y
annihilates
\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{1}}
, it annihilates the vector (
23
). This gives the relation

0=\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}}\Big(Yf_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes v_{i}^{a-j}+(-1)^{\bar{r}+\bar{j}+\bar{1}}f_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes Yv_{i}^{a-j}\Big).

Projecting this relation to the subspace
V^{*}\otimes V[a-j]
gives

(25)

0=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}}Yf_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes v_{i}^{a-j}+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j+1}}(-1)^{\bar{s}+\bar{j}}f_{i}^{-a+j-1+1}\otimes Yv_{i}^{a-j+1}

where we interpret each
v_{i}^{a+1}
as the zero vector. The decomposition

\mathcal{B}^{a-j}=\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-j+1}\sqcup(\mathcal{B}^{a-j}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-j+1})

established in the proof of Lemma
3.10
allows equation (
25
) to be rewritten as

0=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-j+1}}Yf_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes v_{i}^{a-j}+\\
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{Y,a-j+1}}Yf_{i}^{-a+j+1}\otimes v_{i}^{a-j}+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}^{a-j+1}}(-1)^{\bar{s}+\bar{j}}f_{i}^{-a+j-1+1}\otimes Yv_{i}^{a-j+1}.

The definition of the basis
\mathcal{B}^{a-j}
implies that the first sum and the sum of the second two sums vanish independently; the former is the claimed vanishing.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

Proposition
3.11
is surprising in view of a conjecture for the TQFTs constructed from
U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
at a root of unity
[
BCGPM16
, Conjecture 6.4]
, which states that non-generic tori state spaces are not concentrated in degree zero. We expect that the method of proof of Proposition
3.11
can be applied in the setting of
U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
to disprove this conjecture.

Next, we compute the action of the mapping class group
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
of
\Sigma
on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{S}})
. Restrict attention to the case
\omega=0
, so that
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
fixes
\omega
. Use the basis (
20
) of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{S}})
and its dual basis (
21
) and drop
n
from the notation, since it is zero.

In general, let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\omega=0,\mathcal{L})
be a decorated surface without marked points and trivial cohomology class and
f:\Sigma\rightarrow\Sigma
an orientation preserving diffeomorphism. Consider the endomorphism

N_{f}=\mathsf{V}(L_{f_{*}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}}\circ M_{f}):\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})\rightarrow\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}}),

where
M_{f}:(\Sigma,\omega,\mathcal{L})\rightarrow(\Sigma,\omega,f_{*}\mathcal{L})
is the mapping cylinder on
f
and
L_{f_{*}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}}:(\Sigma,\omega,f_{*}\mathcal{L})\rightarrow(\Sigma,\omega,\mathcal{L})
is the decorated cobordism
(\Sigma\times[0,1],\pi_{\Sigma}^{*}\omega,0)
, as defined in
[
BCGPM16
, §3.3.1]
. The assignment
f\mapsto N_{f}
defines a projective representation of the mapping class group of
\Sigma
on
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
:

N_{f}\circ N_{g}=\delta^{-\mu(f_{*}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L},g^{-1}_{*}\mathcal{L})}N_{f\circ g}.

Here
\mu
denotes the Maslov index.

Retuning to the case of the torus, fix an oriented meridian
m
and oriented longitude
l
of
\Sigma
with intersection number
m\cdot l=1
. The elements
T
and
S
given by Dehn twist along
m
and the map which sends
m
to
l
and
l
to
-m
, respectively, generate the mapping class group of
\Sigma
. Take for the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}=\mathbb{R}\cdot[m]
. With this choice, we have
\mu(S_{*}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})=0
and
\mu(T_{*}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})=0
, as in the proof of
[
BCGPM16
, Theorem 6.28]
. It follows that
N_{S}=\mathsf{V}(M_{S})
and
N_{T}=\mathsf{V}(M_{T})
when acting on either
\mathcal{P}_{n,x}
or
\mathcal{P}_{n,x}
. In other words, there are no corrections by the Maslov index.

Following the computations in the proof of Proposition
3.9
, the composition
[\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x}
gives an
S^{3}
containing the core of
\eta
as a
P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}
-colored unknot with a coupon labeled by
x_{0,\bar{0}}
. Using this, we compute

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x})={\mathscr{D}}^{-1}(q-q^{-1})^{-1}.

Similarly, we compute
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x})=0
. It follows that
[\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]
is dual to
{\mathscr{D}}(q-q^{-1})N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x}
. However, from the proof of Proposition
3.9
,
\mathcal{P}
is dual to
[\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]
,
whence
N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x}={\mathscr{D}}^{-1}(q-q^{-1})^{-1}\mathcal{P}
.
Similar computations give
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P})=0
and

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P})={\mathscr{D}}^{-2}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0)_{\bar{0}}}(x_{0,\bar{0}})(-1)(\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(1,0))_{\bar{0}}))^{-1}=1.

Hence,
N_{S}\mathcal{P}
is dual to
[S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]
. Since
\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})\mathcal{P}_{x}
is also dual to
[S\overline{\eta}_{\emptyset}]
, we have
N_{S}\mathcal{P}=\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})\mathcal{P}_{x}
. Continuing, a direct calculation gives
\theta_{P(0)_{\bar{0}}}=\operatorname{Id}_{P(0)_{\bar{0}}}+(q-q^{-1})x_{0,\bar{0}}
. It follows that
N_{T}\mathcal{P}_{x}=\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
N_{T}\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}+(q-q^{-1})\mathcal{P}_{x}
.

Theorem 3.13
.

In the basis
\{\mathcal{P}_{x},\mathcal{P}\}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{S}})
, the mapping class group action is given by

S=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})\\
-\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})^{-1}&0\end{array}\right),\qquad T=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&q-q^{-1}\\
0&1\end{array}\right).

Moreover, this representation is faithful modulo its center.

Proof.

We compute
(ST)^{3}=-\sqrt{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
S^{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, so that we have a projective representation of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{S}})
.
It follows that
\tilde{S}=-\sqrt{-1}S
and
T
generate a (non-projective) representation of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Setting
A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&q-q^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, we have
A\tilde{S}A^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
ATA^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, that is,
A
defines an isomorphism from the representation determined by
\tilde{S}
and
T
to the fundamental representation, which is faithful.
∎

3.4.
The
4
-punctured sphere and the Alexander polynomial

In this section we briefly explain a connection between
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
and the Alexander polynomial. Incarnations of the Alexander polynomial in quantum topology are well-known and include Reshetikhin–Turaev-type constructions from quantizations of
\mathfrak{sl}(n|n)
,
n\geq 1
,
[
KS91
]
and
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)

[
Res92
,
Vir06
,
Sar15
]
. The point we wish to emphasize is that the Alexander polynomial emerges from the TQFT
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
in much the same way as the Jones polynomial emerges from
SU(2)
Chern–Simons theory
[
Wit89a
, §4]
.

Fix
(\alpha,a)\in(\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{C}
. Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the decorated
2
-sphere with four
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}
-colored points, exactly two of which are positively oriented. There is a unique compatible cohomology class
\omega
.

Proposition 3.14
.

The vector space
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})\simeq\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{S}})
is two dimensional.

Proof.

Set
V=V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}
. By Proposition
1.18
, the vector space
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
is spanned by
\omega
-compatible
\mathcal{D}^{q}
-colorings of the ribbon graph
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
in
B^{3}
consisting of a single coupon with four legs. The coupon is therefore colored by an element of

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(\mathbb{C},V\otimes V\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*})\simeq\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(P(0,0)_{\bar{0}})\simeq\mathbb{C}[x_{0,0,\bar{0}}]/\penalty\langle x_{0,0,\bar{0}}^{2}\rangle,

the first isomorphism following from equation (
15
) and the second from Proposition
2.6
. Hence, the dimension of
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
is at most two.

Given
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
, denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}=(B^{3},\Gamma_{\epsilon},\omega,0)\in\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
and
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}=(S^{3}\setminus\mathring{B}^{3},\Theta_{\epsilon},\omega,0)\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}({\mathcal{S}})
the vectors depicted by

\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}=\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-36.0,12.0){\mbox{\tiny$c_{V,V}^{\epsilon}$}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}=\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-3.69885pt\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\psfig{figure,height}}\end{array}\put(-42.0,35.0){\mbox{\tiny$c_{V,V}^{\epsilon}$}}.

Linear independence of the vectors
\{\mathcal{M}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{-}\}
follows by computing

\langle\mathcal{M}_{+}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{+}\rangle\neq 0,\qquad\langle\mathcal{M}_{-}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{+}\rangle=0,\qquad\langle\mathcal{M}_{+}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{-}\rangle=0,\qquad\langle\mathcal{M}_{-}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{-}\rangle\neq 0.

The vanishings follow from the observation that the CGP invariant of the disjoint union of two
V
-colored unknots in
S^{3}
is zero. The non-vanishings follow from the fact that the CGP invariant of a
V
-colored Hopf link in
S^{3}
is non-zero; see Lemma
2.10
.
∎

Let
\{v,v^{\prime}\}
be the weight basis of
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}
from Section
2.3.2
. In the basis

\{v\otimes v,v\otimes v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\otimes v,v^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime}\}\subset V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}},

we compute

c_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}},V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}}=\left(\begin{matrix}q^{-2\alpha a}&0&0&0\\
0&0&q^{-2\alpha a+\alpha}&0\\
0&q^{-2\alpha a+\alpha}&q^{-2\alpha a+\alpha}(q^{\alpha}-q^{-\alpha})&0\\
0&0&0&-q^{-2\alpha a+2\alpha}\end{matrix}\right).

A direct calculation then shows that

q^{2\alpha a-\alpha}c_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}},V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}}-q^{-(2\alpha a-\alpha)}c_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}},V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}}^{-1}=(q^{\alpha}-q^{-\alpha})\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}}.

Writing
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{Id}}\in\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
for the vector obtained by coloring
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
with
\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}}
, the previous equation becomes the linear relation

q^{2\alpha a-\alpha}\mathcal{M}_{+}+q^{-(2\alpha a-\alpha)}\mathcal{M}_{-}=(q^{\alpha}-q^{-\alpha})\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{Id}}

in
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
. It follows from this equation that, upon renormalization,
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
defines an invariant of framed oriented links in
S^{3}
that satisfies the Conway skein relation, as stated in
[
Vir06
, Eqn. (24)]
, specialized to
t^{2}=q^{\alpha}
. More precisely, recall from the proof of Theorem
2.14
that
\theta_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}}=q^{-2\alpha a+\alpha}\operatorname{Id}_{V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}}
. Then
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(L)=(q^{2\alpha a-\alpha})^{\textnormal{wr}(L)}F^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(L)
, where
\textnormal{wr}(L)
is the writhe of
L
, is an invariant of
V(\alpha,a)_{\bar{0}}
-colored oriented links which satisfies the Conway skein relation.

In
[
BCGPM16
, §6.7]
, the TQFT constructed from the relative modular category of weight
U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))
-modules for
q
a primitive fourth root of unity was shown to encode the multivariable Alexander polynomial by arguments similar to those above. Moreover, it was shown that this TQFT evaluates to the canonically normalized Reidemeister torsion on closed
3
-manifolds. We expect these arguments to generalize to the present setting without significant change, thereby connecting
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
with Reidemeister torsion. We leave a detailed verification to future work.

4.
A TQFT for root of unity
q

We work in the setting of Section
2.7
. Fix a positive integer
r\geq 2
. Set
\hbar=\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{r}
if
r
is odd and
\hbar=\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{r}
if
r
is even. Consider
\mathcal{D}^{q}
with the relative modular structures of Theorems
2.20
and
2.22
, so that

\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}&\mbox{if $r$ is odd,}\\
\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{C}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}&\mbox{if $r$ is even,}\end{cases}\qquad\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z},\qquad\mathsf{X}=\begin{cases}\{(\bar{0},\bar{0}),(\bar{\frac{1}{2}},\bar{0})\}&\mbox{if $r$ is odd,}\\
\{(\bar{0},\bar{0})\}&\mbox{if $r$ is even}\end{cases}

and
\Delta_{\pm}=\pm r
. Fix
{\mathscr{D}}=r\sqrt{-1}
so that
\delta=-\sqrt{-1}
. The braiding of
\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is determined by

\gamma:\mathsf{Z}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\},\qquad((n_{1},n^{\prime}_{1},\bar{p}_{1}),(n_{2},n^{\prime}_{2},\bar{p}_{2}))\mapsto(-1)^{\bar{p}_{1}\bar{p}_{2}}.

In this section we study the TQFT
\mathcal{Z}:\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}\rightarrow\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
associated to
\mathcal{D}^{q}
by Theorem
1.12
. In the notation of Section
1.5
, we fix

g_{0}=\begin{cases}(\bar{\frac{1}{2}},\bar{0})&\mbox{if }r=2\mbox{ or $r$ is odd},\\
(\bar{0},\bar{0})&\mbox{if $r\geq 4$ is even,}\end{cases}\qquad V_{g_{0}}=\begin{cases}V(1,0)_{\bar{0}}&\mbox{if }r=2\mbox{ or $r$ is odd},\\
V(2,0)_{\bar{0}}&\mbox{if $r\geq 4$ is even.}\end{cases}

As in Section
3
, the invariant
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{M})
of a morphism
\mathcal{M}:\emptyset\rightarrow\emptyset
in
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
depends holomorphically on the cohomology class
\omega
appearing in the definition of
\mathcal{M}
.

4.1.
The state space of a generic surface of genus at least one

We modify the arguments of Section
3.1
to construct a spanning set of the state space of a surface of genus at least one.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\omega)
be a decorated connected surface without marked points and underlying surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 1
. Since
\mathsf{X}\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is again a subgroup, Lemma
3.1
continues to hold. Definition
3.2
applies in the present setting, where now
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
and
\sigma
are as in the root of unity case. Fix a lift
\tilde{\omega}\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z})
of
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
and let

\mathfrak{C}^{(r)}_{k}=\{\mbox{degree $k$ colorings of }\widetilde{\Gamma}\mid\mathsf{Col}(e_{i})\in\tilde{\omega}(m_{e_{i}})+\{(j,k)_{\bar{0}}\}_{0\leq j,k\leq r-1},\;i=1,\dots,g\}

and
\mathfrak{C}^{(r)}=\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathsf{Z}}\mathfrak{C}^{(r)}_{k}
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\omega)
be a decorated connected surface without marked points and underlying surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 1
. If
r=2
, assume that
\tilde{\omega}(e_{1})+g_{0}\not\in\mathsf{X}
. The set
\{v_{c}\mid c\in\mathfrak{C}^{(r)}_{k}\}
spans
\mathcal{Z}_{-k}({\mathcal{S}})
. Moreover,
\mathcal{Z}_{-k}({\mathcal{S}})
is trivial unless
k=(0,d,\bar{d})
for some
d\in[-g,g]\cap r\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

The proof is a variation of that of Theorem
3.3
. Note that if
r\neq 2
, then
g_{0}=0
and the condition
\tilde{\omega}(e_{1})+g_{0}\not\in\mathsf{X}
holds automatically for all generic
\omega
. The main difference from Theorem
3.3
is that there are now
r^{2}
choices for the color of each edge
e_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,g
. The colors of the edges
\{f_{i}\}
and
e_{g+1}
of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
are determined by a tuple
(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{2g-3},\mu)\in\{0,1\}^{\times 2g-2}
through the same recursive system of equations as in the proof of Theorem
3.3
. Writing
k=(nr,n^{\prime}r,\bar{t})
, the colors of the remaining the edges
e_{1}^{\prime}
and
e_{g+1}^{\prime}
are determined by the equations

(\alpha_{1},a_{1})_{\bar{p}_{1}}+g_{0}=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime})_{\bar{p}_{1}^{\prime}}+\epsilon\delta,

(\alpha_{g+1}^{\prime},a_{g+1}^{\prime})_{\bar{p}_{g+1}^{\prime}}+(nr,n^{\prime}r)_{\bar{t}}=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime})_{\bar{p}_{1}^{\prime}}

(\alpha_{g+1}^{\prime},a_{g+1}^{\prime})_{\bar{p}_{g+1}^{\prime}}+\epsilon^{\prime}\delta=(\alpha_{g+1},a_{g+1})_{\bar{p}_{g+1}}+g_{0}.

Here
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. These equations hold if and only if

nr=0,\qquad n^{\prime}r=\epsilon-\epsilon^{\prime}-\mu-\sum_{j=1}^{2g-3}(-1)^{j}\epsilon_{j},\qquad\bar{t}=\bar{\epsilon}+\bar{\epsilon}^{\prime}+\bar{\mu}+\sum_{j=1}^{2g-3}\bar{\epsilon}_{j}.

The first equation implies that
n=0
while the second implies that
r
divides
\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon-\mu-\sum_{j=1}^{2g-3}\epsilon_{j}
.
∎

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{(r)}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{C}^{(r)}_{k}
the subset of colorings of degree
k
with
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}=0
, in the notation of the proof of Theorem
4.1
. Set
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{(r)}=\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathsf{Z}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{(r)}_{k}
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Work in the setting of Theorem
4.1
. For each
d\in[-(g-1),g-1]\cap r\mathbb{Z}
, the vectors
\{v_{c}\mid c\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{(r)}_{(0,d,\bar{d})}\}
form a linearly independent subset of
\mathcal{Z}_{-(0,d,\bar{d})}({\mathcal{S}})
of cardinality
r^{2g}{2g-2\choose g-1-|d|}
.

Proof.

This is proved in the same way as Proposition
3.4
.
∎

4.2.
A Verlinde formula

Consider now the analogue of Section
3.2
for
q
a root of unity.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\},\omega)
be a decorated connected surface of genus
g\geq 0
. Let
(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
and consider
{\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})}=(\Sigma\times S^{1},T=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\times S^{1},\omega\oplus(\bar{\beta},\bar{b}))
. Assume that all colors of
{\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})}
are in
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})
can be computed as in Section
3.2
. The main difference is that Kirby colors are now formal linear combination of
r^{2}
simple objects and
\zeta=-r^{2}
. Taking this into account, we find

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})={\mathscr{D}}^{-2g-2}r^{4g}\sum_{i,j=0}^{r-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\beta_{0}+i,b_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}})^{2-2g}S^{\prime}(\{X_{i}\},\Omega_{\beta}),

where
(\beta_{0},b_{0})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
is a chosen lift of
(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
. Write the color of
p_{k}
as
X_{k}=V(\mu_{k},m_{k})_{\bar{q}_{k}}
. With this notation, we have

(26)

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})=(-1)^{g+1+n+\bar{q}}r^{2g-2}\sum_{i,j=0}^{r-1}q^{-2((\beta_{0}+i)\bar{m}+(b_{0}+j)\bar{\mu})}(q^{\beta_{0}+i}-q^{-\beta_{0}-i})^{2g-2+n}.

By holomorphicity, this equality holds whenever
{\mathcal{S}}
is admissible.

Define the generating function of
\mathsf{Z}
-graded dimensions of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
by

\dim_{(t_{1},t_{2},s)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\sum_{(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})\in\mathsf{Z}}(-1)^{\bar{p}}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}_{(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})}({\mathcal{S}})t_{1}^{n}t_{2}^{n^{\prime}}s^{\bar{p}}.

We can now state the Verlinde formula in the present setting.

Theorem 4.3
.

(1)

If
r
is odd, then
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})=\dim_{(q^{-2rb},q^{-2r\beta},1)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
.

(2)

If
r
is even, then
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})=\dim_{(q^{-4rb},q^{-2r\beta},1)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
.

Proof.

This is a direct modification of the proof of Theorem
3.5
. For example, when
r
is odd, the appearance of the pairing

\psi:\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times},\qquad((\bar{\alpha},\bar{a}),(n,n^{\prime},\bar{s}))\mapsto q^{-2(2n\bar{a}+n^{\prime}\bar{\alpha})r}

leads to the replacement of
q^{-2\beta b_{i}}
with
q^{-2(\beta b_{i}+b\delta_{i})}
in the second equality of computation from the proof of Theorem
3.5
.
∎

When
r
is odd, the right hand side of the equality in Theorem
4.3
can be written as

\dim_{(q^{-2rb},q^{-2r\beta},1)}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\sum_{(n,n^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\chi(\mathcal{Z}_{(n,n^{\prime},\bullet)}({\mathcal{S}}))q^{-2r(\overline{\beta}n^{\prime}+\overline{b}n)}.

There is a similar formula when
r
is even.

Example 4.4
.

When
n=0
, equation (
26
) becomes

\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})=(-1)^{g+1}r^{2g-1}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}(q^{\beta_{0}+i}-q^{-\beta_{0}-i})^{2g-2}.

Assume that
g\geq 1
and
r
is odd. Theorem
4.1
implies
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})=\lim_{\bar{\beta}\rightarrow\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})
. To compute the limit, recall the identities

(27)

\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\cos(a+nb)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\sin(\frac{Nb}{2})}{\sin(\frac{b}{2})}\cos\left(\frac{(N-1)b}{2}+a\right)&\mbox{if }b\notin 2\pi\mathbb{Z},\\
N\cos a&\mbox{if }b\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\end{cases}

and

(28)

\cos^{2N}\theta=\frac{1}{2^{2N}}{2N\choose N}+\frac{(-1)^{N}}{2^{2N-1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(-1)^{k}{2N\choose k}\cos\left(2(N-k)\theta\right)

where
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. See
[
PBM86
, Eqn. (4.4.1.5)]
and
[
PBM86
, Eqn. (I.1.9)]
, respectively. We compute

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{2g-1}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\Big(2\sin\Big(\frac{2\pi(\frac{1}{4}+i)}{r}\Big)\Big)^{2g-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{2g}{2g-2\choose g-1}+(-1)^{g-1}2r^{2g-1}\sum_{k=0}^{g-2}(-1)^{k}{2g-2\choose k}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\cos\left(\frac{4\pi(g-1-k)(\frac{1}{4}+i)}{r}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{2g}{2g-2\choose g-1}+(-1)^{g-1}2r^{2g-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=0\\
r\mid g-1-k\end{subarray}}^{g-2}(-1)^{k}{2g-2\choose k}r\cos\left(\frac{\pi(g-1-k)}{r}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{2g}{2g-2\choose g-1}+(-1)^{g-1}2r^{2g}\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{\left\lfloor{\frac{g-1}{r}}\right\rfloor}(-1)^{g-1-n^{\prime}r}{2g-2\choose g-1-n^{\prime}r}\cos(n^{\prime}\pi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r^{2g}\sum_{n^{\prime}=-\left\lfloor{\frac{g-1}{r}}\right\rfloor}^{\left\lfloor{\frac{g-1}{r}}\right\rfloor}{2g-2\choose g-1-|n^{\prime}|r}.

The second equality follows from equation (
27
), the third from equation (
28
) and the fourth from writing
g-1-k=n^{\prime}r
. Similarly, we compute

\chi(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}))=\lim_{\bar{\beta}\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}\times S^{1}_{(\bar{\beta},\bar{b})})=r^{2g-1}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\left(2\sin\left(\frac{2\pi i}{r}\right)\right)^{2g-2}=r^{2g}{2g-2\choose g-1},

the final equality following from
[
PBM86
, Eqn. (4.4.2.1)]
.

The same expressions hold for
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
and
\chi(\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}}))
when
r
is even.
∎

Corollary 4.5
.

Work in the setting of Theorem
3.3
. The vectors
\{v_{c}\mid c\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{(r)}\}
are a basis of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
. In particular,
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is supported in degrees
(0,d,\bar{d})
with
d\in[-(g-1),g-1]\cap r\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
4.2
, we compute

|\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{(r)}|=\sum_{d\in[(-g-1),g-1)]\cap r\mathbb{Z}}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{(r)}_{(d,\bar{d})}|=\sum_{d\in[(-g-1),g-1)]\cap r\mathbb{Z}}r^{2g}{2g-2\choose g-1-|d|},

which is equal to
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
by Example
4.4
.
∎

4.3.
The state space of the torus with non-generic cohomology class

Let
\Sigma=S^{1}\times S^{1}
and
{\mathcal{S}}=(\Sigma,\omega,\mathcal{L})
. Let
\eta=B^{2}\times S^{1}
with core
\Gamma=\{0\}\times S^{1}
. By Corollary
4.5
, when
\omega
is generic,
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is concentrated in
\mathsf{Z}
-degree zero and is
r^{2}
dimensional. The next result gives a non-generic counterpart of this result.

Proposition 4.6
.

Assume that there exists an oriented simple closed curve
\gamma
in
\Sigma
such that
\omega(\gamma)\in\mathsf{X}\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is concentrated in
\mathsf{Z}
-degree
0
and is of dimension
r^{2}+1
.

Proof.

Up to diffeomorphism, we may assume that
\omega(m_{\Gamma})\in\mathsf{X}
. For concreteness, suppose that
\omega(m_{\Gamma})=(\bar{0},\bar{0})
; the case in which
r
is odd and
\omega(m_{\Gamma})=(\bar{\frac{1}{2}},\bar{0})
is similar. Given
1\leq i\leq r-1
and
0\leq j\leq r-1
, denote by
\mathcal{M}_{i,j}
,
\mathcal{P}_{j}
and
\mathcal{P}_{j,x}
the vectors in
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
with underlying
3
-manifold
\eta
with core is colored by
V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}
,
P(0,j)_{\bar{0}}
and
P(0,j)_{\bar{0}}
with coupon
x_{0,j,\bar{0}}
, respectively. The set

(29)

\{\mathcal{M}_{i,j},\mathcal{P}_{j}\mid 1\leq i\leq r-1,\;0\leq j\leq r-1\}\cup\{\mathcal{P}_{x}=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\mathcal{P}_{j,x}\}

spans
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
, as follows from Propositions
1.18
and
2.6
. For linear independence,
consider first the
{\rm CGP}
-pairings of
[\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]
, a negatively oriented copy of
\eta
with meridian colored by
V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}
, with the vectors in question.

Denote by
[\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]
a negatively oriented copy of
\eta
with meridian colored by
V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}
. The decorated manifold underlying
[\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]\circ\mathcal{M}_{k,l,\bar{0}}
has a surgery presentation in
S^{3}
given by two parallel knots of opposite orientation labeled by
V(i,j)
and
V(k,l)
encircled by an
\Omega_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})}
-colored unknot, for any generic
(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})
. Using this, we compute

\displaystyle{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\eta_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]\circ\mathcal{M}_{k,l,\bar{0}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{i,k}\delta_{j,l}\zeta}{{\mathscr{D}}^{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(i,j)_{\bar{0}})}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}^{*}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{\operatorname{coev}}}_{V(i,+j)_{\bar{0}}}\circ\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{i,k}\delta_{j,l}\zeta(q-q^{-1})\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(i,j)_{\bar{0}})}{{\mathscr{D}}^{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{d}}(V(\alpha_{0}+i,a_{0}+j)_{\bar{0}})}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}(x_{0,0,\bar{0}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{i,k}\delta_{j,l}.

The first equality follows from the relative modularity condition (
2
), the second from Lemma
2.12
and the third from Lemma
2.11
. The pairing
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{k})
vanishes since it is proportional to the modified trace of
\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})},V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}\otimes P(0,k)_{\bar{0}}}
, which vanishes by part (
3
) of Lemma
2.19
. For similar reasons, we have
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{k,x})=0
. It follows that
\{\mathcal{M}_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
are linearly independent and are linearly independent from
\{\mathcal{P}_{k},\mathcal{P}_{k,x}\}_{k}
.

Continuing, denote by
[\overline{\eta}_{-l}]
a negatively oriented copy of
\eta
with meridian colored by
\varepsilon(0,l)_{\bar{0}}
. Using Lemma
2.11
and part (
2
) of Lemma
2.19
, we compute

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-l}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{k})={\mathscr{D}}^{-2}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0,k-l)}\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})},P(0,k-l)_{\bar{0}}}=\delta_{k,l}

and

\displaystyle{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-l}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{k,x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathscr{D}}^{-2}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0,k-l)}\left(x_{0,k-l,\bar{0}}\circ\Phi_{\Omega_{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{a})},P(0,k-l)_{\bar{0}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\mathscr{D}}^{-2}(q-q^{-1})r^{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}}(x^{2}_{0,0,\bar{0}})\delta_{k,l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Hence,
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}_{k}
are linearly independent and are linearly independent from
\{\mathcal{P}_{k,x}\}_{k}
. We claim that
\{\mathcal{P}_{k,x}\}_{k}
spans a one dimensional subspace of
\mathsf{V}({\mathcal{S}})
. By Proposition
2.6
, the endomorphisms
a_{j,\bar{p}}^{-},a_{j+1,\bar{p}+\bar{1}}^{+}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(P(0,j)_{\bar{p}}\oplus P(0,j+1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}})
satisfy
[a_{j,\bar{p}}^{-},a_{j+1,\bar{p}+\bar{1}}^{+}]=x_{0,j,\bar{p}}\oplus x_{0,j+1,\bar{p}+\bar{1}}
.
The vector defined by coloring the core with
P(0,j)_{\bar{p}}\oplus P(0,j+1)_{\bar{p}+\bar{1}}
and coupon
x_{0,j,\bar{p}}\oplus x_{0,j+1,\bar{p}+\bar{1}}
is zero, since the coupon is a commutator. On the other hand, this vector is equal to
\mathcal{P}_{j,x}-\mathcal{P}_{j+1,x}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{P}_{j,x}=\mathcal{P}_{k,x}
for all
j,k
. Finally, in the notation of Section
3.3
, we have

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([S\overline{\eta}_{-l}]\circ\mathcal{P}_{j,x})={\mathscr{D}}^{-1}\operatorname{\mathsf{t}}_{P(\frac{(j-l)\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0)_{\bar{0}}}(x_{\frac{(j-l)\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},0,\bar{0}})={\mathscr{D}}^{-1}(q-q^{-1})^{-1}.

for all
j,k
. In particular,
\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\mathcal{P}_{j,x}\neq 0
.

Finally, to prove that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
is concentrated in
\mathsf{Z}
-degree
0
, recall from Proposition
1.17
that
\mathcal{Z}_{-k}({\mathcal{S}})
,
k\in\mathsf{Z}
, is spanned by finite projective
\mathcal{D}^{q}
-colorings of the ribbon graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
. In genus
1
, the coupons of
\Gamma^{\prime}
are represented by a composition

V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}\otimes V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}^{*}\simeq P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}\rightarrow P\rightarrow V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(k)\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*}\simeq P(nr,nr^{\prime})_{\bar{p}}

or

P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}^{\otimes 2}\simeq P(0,1)_{\bar{1}}\oplus P(0,0)_{\bar{0}}^{\oplus 2}\oplus P(0,-1)_{\bar{1}}\rightarrow P\rightarrow V_{g_{0}}\otimes\sigma(k)\otimes V_{g_{0}}^{*}\simeq P(nr,nr^{\prime})_{\bar{p}},

where
P
is a projective indecomposable of degree
(\overline{0},\overline{0})
and
k=(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})
. By Proposition
2.6
, if either of these compositions is non-zero, then
k=0
.
∎

We claim that the basis dual to (
29
) is

(30)

\{[\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}],[\overline{\eta}_{-j}]\mid 1\leq i\leq r-1,\;0\leq j\leq r-1\}\cup\{s=\frac{\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})}{r}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}[S\overline{\eta}_{-j}]\}.

Most of this statement was verified in the proof of Proposition
4.6
. That
s
pairs trivially with each
\mathcal{P}_{j}
follows from Lemma
2.11
. Using equation (
16
), we compute

\displaystyle{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(s\circ\mathcal{M}_{i,j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})}{r}\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([S\overline{\eta}_{-l}]\circ\mathcal{M}_{i,j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})}{r}{\mathscr{D}}^{-1}\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}q^{-2il}(q^{i}-q^{-i})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

because
q^{-2i}\neq 1
is an
r
th
root of unity. Similarly, part (
1
) of Lemma
2.19
implies that
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-l}]\circ\mathcal{M}_{i,j})=0
for all
i,j,l
. This establishes the claim.

Next, we compute the action of the mapping class group of
\Sigma
on
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
when
\omega=0
using the basis (
29
) and its dual basis (
30
). As in Section
3.3
, we take
\mathcal{L}=\mathbb{R}\cdot[m]
and find that there are no Maslov corrections. Using Lemmas
2.10
and
2.11
, we compute

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{M}_{k,l})=-{\mathscr{D}}^{-1}q^{-2(il+jk)+i+k}

and

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-j}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{M}_{k,l})={\mathscr{D}}^{-1}\frac{q^{-2jk}}{q^{k}-q^{-k}}.

The calculations checking that (
30
) is a dual basis show that
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(s\circ N_{S}\mathcal{M}_{k,l})=0
. Using Lemmas
2.10
and
2.11
, we compute

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{l})=-{\mathscr{D}}^{-1}q^{-2il}(q^{i}-q^{-i})

and
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-j}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{l})=0
. Using Lemma
2.19
(
2
), we find

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(s\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{l})=\frac{\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})}{r}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([S\overline{\eta}_{-j}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{l})\\
=\frac{\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})}{r},

the only contribution coming from the
j=l
summand. Part (
3
) of Lemma
2.19
implies
{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x})=0
while Lemma
2.11
implies

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([\overline{\eta}_{-j}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x})=-{\mathscr{D}}^{-1}\frac{r}{q-q^{-1}}.

Finally, Lemma
2.10
and the fact that
x_{0,k,\bar{0}}^{2}=0
for all
k
gives

{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}(s\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x})=\frac{\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})}{r}\sum_{j,l=0}^{r-1}{\rm CGP}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}([S\overline{\eta}_{-j}]\circ N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{l,x})=0.

The above calculations prove the first part of the following result.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a connected decorated surface of genus one without marked points and with non-generic cohomology class. The action of the mapping class group
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})
in the basis (
29
) is determined by the equations

N_{S}\mathcal{M}_{i,j}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{r}\sum^{r-1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l=0\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}q^{-2(k(j-\frac{1}{2})+i(l-\frac{1}{2}))}\mathcal{M}_{k,l}-\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{r}\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{q^{-2il}}{q^{i}-q^{-i}}\mathcal{P}_{l},

N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{j}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}(q-q^{-1})}{r}\mathcal{P}_{x}+\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l=0\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}^{r-1}q^{-jk}(q^{k}-q^{-k})\mathcal{M}_{k,l},

N_{S}\mathcal{P}_{x}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\mathcal{P}_{l}

and

N_{T}\mathcal{M}_{i,j}=q^{-2i(j-\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{M}_{i,j},\qquad N_{T}\mathcal{P}_{j}=\mathcal{P}_{j}+\frac{(q-q^{-1})}{r}\mathcal{P}_{x},\qquad N_{T}\mathcal{P}_{x}=\mathcal{P}_{x}.

In particular, the Dehn twist acts with infinite order.

Proof.

It remains to compute the action of the Dehn twist
T
. Recall from the proof of Theorem
2.14
that
\theta_{V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}=q^{-2i(j-\frac{1}{2})}\operatorname{Id}_{V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}}
. A similar computation gives
\theta_{P(0,j)_{\bar{0}}}=\operatorname{Id}_{P(0,j)_{\bar{0}}}+(q-q^{-1})x_{0,j,\bar{0}}
. The action of
T
is therefore as stated. The final statement follows from the observation that
N^{k}_{T}\mathcal{P}_{j}=\mathcal{P}_{j}+k\frac{q-q^{-1}}{r}\mathcal{P}_{x}
,
k\geq 1
.
∎

5.
TQFTs from integral modules

The relative modular structures on
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
constructed in Sections
2.8.1
and
2.8.2
give integral counterparts of the TQFTs of Sections
3
and
4
. Since the computations for
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
are straightforward modifications of those for
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
, we limit our discussion to a summary.

5.1.
Arbitrary
q

Work in the setting of Section
2.8.1
and give
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
the relative modular structure of Theorem
2.23
.
Fix
{\mathscr{D}}=\sqrt{-1}
. The braiding of
\mathsf{V}\mathsf{ect}^{\mathsf{Z}\operatorname{-}\textnormal{gr}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is determined by

\gamma:\mathsf{Z}\times\mathsf{Z}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\},\qquad((n_{1},n^{\prime}_{1},\bar{p}_{1}),(n_{2},n^{\prime}_{2},\bar{p}_{2}))\mapsto(-1)^{\bar{p}_{1}\bar{p}_{2}}.

Since each
\Theta(\bar{\alpha})
,
\bar{\alpha}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\setminus\mathsf{X}
, is a singleton, it is straightforward to verify that equation (
19
) and the Verlinde formula (Theorem
3.5
) hold as written for
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
.
A priori
, the difference between the free realisation groups of
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{q}
, being
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}=\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
, could lead to significant differences between the state spaces of generic surfaces. However, we have seen in Corollary
3.7
that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})
is concentrated in integral degrees and spanned by graphs colored by integral modules. For this reason, when
{\mathcal{S}}
is of genus
g\geq 1
, we obtain canonical isomorphisms

(31)

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}},(0,d,\bar{d})}({\mathcal{S}})\simeq\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q},(d,\bar{d})}({\mathcal{S}}),\qquad d\in[-(g-1),g-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}

which define an isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}({\mathcal{S}})\simeq\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}({\mathcal{S}})
which is compatible with the group homomorphism
\mathsf{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
,
(n,n^{\prime},\bar{p})\mapsto(n^{\prime},\bar{p})
. The computations of Sections
3.3
and
3.4
go through with only obvious modifications and no changes to the end results.

5.2.
q
a root of unity

Work in the setting of Section
2.8.2
with
{\mathscr{D}}
and
\gamma
defined as in Section
4
. The TQFT
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
has the same properties as the TQFT
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q}}
of Section
4
, with analogues of Theorems
4.1
and
4.3
, equation (
26
) and Proposition
4.6
holding with only obvious changes. The isomorphism (
31
) again holds in the current setting, with the additional assumption that
d
is divisible by
r
. The computations of Sections
4.3
and
3.4
go through with only obvious modifications and no changes to the end results.

6.
Comparison with supergroup Chern–Simons and Wess–Zumino–Witten theories coupled to background flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connections

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a modular
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-category relative to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X})
. Recently, there has been significant interest in the physics literature in constructing quantum field theories which realize (a differential graded or derived enhancement of) the TQFT
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}

[
GHN+21
,
CDGG21
,
CGP21
]
. Such a quantum field theory is expected to admit
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
as a group of global symmetries so that it can be coupled to background flat
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-connections, thereby producing local invariants of
3
-manifolds with flat
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
-connection. With this problem in mind, in
[
CDGG21
]
a physical quantum field theory
\mathcal{T}^{A}_{n,r}
was constructed as a topological
A
-twist of
3
d
\mathcal{N}=4
Chern–Simons-matter theory with gauge group
SU(n)
at level
r-n
which conjecturally realizes the TQFT
\mathcal{Z}_{U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(n))}
associated to the category of weight
U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(n))
-modules at a primitive
2r
th
root of unity. Using sophisticated techniques from quantum field theory and vertex operators algebras, a number of calculations were made for the theory
\mathcal{T}^{A}_{2,r}
and the results were shown to match known properties of
\mathcal{Z}_{U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(2))}
, thereby presenting evidence for the conjecture when
n=2
.

In this section we argue that the TQFTs
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
of Sections
5.1
and
5.2
have comparatively simple physical realizations, namely, as
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
and
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theories, respectively. Supergroup Chern–Simons theories have the benefit of being formally similar to Chern–Simons theory with compact gauge group
[
Wit89a
]
, allowing for the import of intuition from the compact case. By work of Kapustin and Saulina
[
KS09
]
, Chern–Simons theories with gauge supergroup arise as
B
-twists of
3
d
\mathcal{N}=4
supersymmetric quantum field theories and, for particular choices of gauge supergroup, are
3
d mirrors of the quantum field theories
\mathcal{T}^{A}_{n,r}
constructed in
[
CDGG21
]
.

6.1.
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory

Let
\mathfrak{pgl}(1|1)
be the quotient of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
by its center
\mathbb{C}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
the quotient of
\mathfrak{pgl}(1|1)
by the Lie ideal
\mathbb{C}\cdot\overline{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
. The Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
is two dimensional, purely odd and abelian. The group
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
acts on
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
with weights
+1
and
-1
. More generally,
GL(2,\mathbb{C})
acts on
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
by Lie superalgebra automorphisms, but only the action of the anti-diagonal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\leq GL(2,\mathbb{C})
lifts to
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
.

Chern–Simons theory with gauge supergroup
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
was studied by Mikhaylov
[
Mik15
]
. Since
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
is purely odd, this theory has a number of peculiarities compared to the compact case, including no quantization of the level and being defined by the Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
without the choice of an associated Lie supergroup. This theory is closely related to a number of other well-known models in physics, including the Rozansky–Witten theory of the cotangent bundle
T^{\vee}\mathbb{C}

[
RW97
]
and the
B
-twist of a
3
d
\mathcal{N}=4
free hypermultiplet
[
KS09
,
Mik15
,
CG19
,
CDGG21
]
. The
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
lifts to a global symmetry of
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory, thereby allowing for the theory to be coupled to background flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connections.

Proposal 6.1
.

For arbitrary
q
, the TQFT
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
described in Section
5.1
is the homological truncation of
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory coupled to flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connections.

At present, there is no mathematical definition of
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory so that Proposal
6.1
cannot be formulated as a theorem. Instead, we offer evidence for the proposal. We identify the grading group
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}=\mathbb{C}/\penalty\frac{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
so that the cohomology classes
\omega\in H^{1}(-;\operatorname{\mathsf{G}})
appearing as decoration data of the category
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{ob}^{\textnormal{ad}}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
can be interpreted as isomorphism classes of flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connections.

•

\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory admits Wilson operators labeled by a link whose components are colored by representations of
\mathfrak{pgl}(1|1)

[
Mik15
, §2.4]
. Indeed, the tensor product of the dynamical
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
-connection (used to define the
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons Lagrangian) with the background
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connection defines a
\mathfrak{pgl}(1|1)
-connection from which Wilson operators can be constructed, analogously to the construction of Wilson operators in Chern–Simons theory with compact gauge group
[
Wit89a
, §2.1]
. In the notation of Section
2.3
, the simple representations of
\mathfrak{pgl}(1|1)
are
\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{p}}
,
(b,\bar{p})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
. The restriction to integral modules,
b\in\mathbb{Z}
, reflects the desired interpretation of the
\varepsilon(0,1)_{\bar{0}}
-labeled Wilson loop as an operator which changes the
\operatorname{spin}^{c}
-structure by a single unit
[
Mik15
, §2.4]
. In the physical approach,
\operatorname{spin}^{c}
-structures enter in the definition of the phase of partition functions. Wilson operators labeled by the projective indecomposables
P(0,b)_{\bar{p}}
of
\mathfrak{pgl}(1|1)
are studied in
[
Mik15
, §5.3.3]
.

•

\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory also admits monodromy operators
[
Mik15
, §2.4]
. These operators take as input a framed link
L
and prescribe the holonomy of the background flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connection along the meridians of
L
. More generally, one can combine this operator with a Wilson operator to obtain a
combined (Wilson/monodromy) operator
. In our set up, the meridian holonomy is captured by the
E
-weights. Note that
E
is not a generator of
\mathfrak{pgl}(1|1)
. For example, coloring a knot by a simple module
V(\alpha,b)_{\bar{p}}\in\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
corresponds to the combined operator with prescribed holonomy
q^{\alpha}\neq 1
and Wilson factor
\varepsilon(0,b)_{\bar{p}}
. When the meridian holonomy is trivial,
q^{\alpha}=1
, so that
\alpha=\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we realize the combined operator as coloring by the projective indecomposable
P(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
with a coupon labeled by its non-zero idempotent. Viewing this data as the projective cover of
\varepsilon(\frac{n\pi\sqrt{-1}}{\hbar},b)_{\bar{p}}
, as in Lemma
2.7
, we see that each simple
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
-module corresponds to a unique combined operator. In particular, enlarging
\mathfrak{pgl}(1|1)
to
U_{q}^{E}(\mathfrak{gl}(1|1))
allows for the incorporation of both Wilson and monodromy operators of
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory.

•

The partition function of trivial circle bundles over surfaces, given by equation (
19
), agrees with
[
Mik15
, Eqns. (5.3), (5.8)]
. Similarly, the graded dimensions of state spaces of generic surfaces (Corollary
3.7
) agrees with the results of Mikhaylov’s formal application of geometric quantization to super Chern–Simons theory
[
Mik15
, Eqns. (5.6-7)]
.

•

That the dimension of the degree zero summand
\mathcal{Z}_{0}({\mathcal{S}})
of the state space of a non-generic torus is two (Proposition
3.9
) agrees with
[
Mik15
, §5.3.1]
. By Proposition
3.11
, the summands
\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\mathcal{S}})
vanish for non-zero
k
. On the other hand, Mikhaylov predicted that the state space of derived
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory contains a factor of
\left(\bigwedge\nolimits^{\bullet}H^{1}({\mathcal{S}};\mathbb{C})\right)[-1]
, the degree zero summand of which is
H^{1}({\mathcal{S}};\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{C}^{2}

[
Mik15
, §5.2]
. In view of the expectation that
\mathcal{Z}
describes the homological truncation of derived
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory, this suggests that the summands
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}H^{1}({\mathcal{S}};\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathbb{C}
,
k=0,2
, appear only at the derived level. It is interesting to note that these summands also appear in the state space of the TQFT of Frohman and Nicas
[
FN91
,
Ker03
]
.

•

That the mapping class group action on the degree zero state space of non-generic tori is projectively isomorphic to the fundamental representation of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
matches with
[
Mik15
, §5.3.1]
.

•

The results of Section
3.4
match physical expectations that
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory recovers the multivariable Alexander polynomial
[
Mik15
, §5.2.2]
.

6.2.
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory

The physical study of Chern–Simons theories with gauge supergroups
U(1|1)
and
GL(1|1)
was initiated by Rozansky and Saleur
[
RS92
,
RS93
,
RS94
]
under the assumption of the existence of a super generalization of the Chern–Simons/Wess–Zumino–Witten correspondence. Mikhaylov studied
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory without reference to Wess–Zumino–Witten theory, viewing it instead as the orbifold of
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory by a finite cyclic group
[
Mik15
, §4.3]
.

The group
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
acts on
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
by Lie algebra automorphisms with weight decomposition

\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)_{-1}=\mathbb{C}\cdot Y,\qquad\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)_{0}=\mathbb{C}\cdot G\oplus\mathbb{C}\cdot E,\qquad\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)_{+1}=\mathbb{C}\cdot X.

This
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action lifts to a global symmetry of Chern–Simons theories with gauge supergroups
GL(1|1)
and
U(1|1)
, allowing each theory to be coupled to flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connections.

Proposal 6.2
.

For
q
a primitive
r
th
root of unity, the TQFT
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}}
described in Section
5.2
is the homological truncation of
U(1|1)
Chern–Simons theory at level
r
coupled to flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-connections.

We outline evidence for this proposal. For concreteness, suppose that
r
is odd. Again, we can identify the grading group
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
with
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

•

Mirroring the discussion from Section
6.1
, the
\mathcal{D}^{q,\textnormal{int}}
-coloring of ribbon graphs used in this paper matches the labelings of the combined Wilson/monodromy operators constructed in
[
Mik15
, §4.5]
. At first sight, there is an ambiguity of whether to view colorings by simple modules of the form
V(i,j)_{\bar{0}}
,
\{0\leq i,j\leq r-1\}
, as Wilson or monodromy operators. (The integrality of the
E
-weights of such modules implies that their classical limits lift to representations of
U(1|1)
, whence can be viewed as labeling Wilson operators.) However, physical arguments suggest that these two operators coincide for such modules
[
MW15
, §3.2]
,
[
Mik15
, §4.5]
.

•

The partition function of trivial circle bundles over surfaces, given by equation (
26
), agrees with
[
RS93
, Eqn. (123)]
. We are not aware of results in the physics literature which compute the dimension of state spaces of generic surfaces, as in Corollary
4.5
.

•

The dimensions of state spaces of non-generic tori, as computed in Proposition
4.6
, agree with
[
Mik15
, §5.4]
and the proposal of Aghaei, Gainutdinov, Pawelkiewicz and Schomerus
[
AGPS18
]
, who construct candidate state spaces of non-generic tori using combinatorial quantization based on the small quantum group of
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
. State spaces of generic tori do not seem to have been studied in the physics literature.

•

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a decorated torus without marked points and with trivial cohomology class. The mapping class group action on
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{S}})
obtained in Theorem
4.7
agrees with the regularized mapping class group action obtained using
U(1|1)
Wess–Zumino–Witten theory
[
RS93
, §3]
. Theorem
4.7
is closely related to the mapping class group actions of
[
Mik15
, §5.4]
and
[
AGPS18
, Eqns. (4.61-3), (4.57-9)]
. Explicitly, the relation between the basis (
29
) and that of
[
Mik15
, §5.4]
is

\mathcal{M}_{i,j}\longleftrightarrow|L_{i,j}\rangle\qquad\mathcal{P}_{j}\longleftrightarrow|L_{j}\rangle\qquad\mathcal{P}_{x}\longleftrightarrow r\cdot v_{0}\otimes|0_{a}\rangle.

Under this correspondence, the only difference in mapping class group actions is that the trigonometric factors
q^{i}-q^{-i}
in Theorem
4.7
appear inverted in
[
Mik15
,
AGPS18
]
.
